## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### How sure, about now and about then

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. Sentence 1 and sentence 2 are both near-certainties. **One is positive and one is negative.
   What are the two forms?**
2. In sentence 3, what does *well* add to *might have*?
3. Sentence 4 avoids a modal altogether. **What does it gain and what does it lose?**
4. **What is the present-time version of each of the four?**
5. Try *"It mustn't have been an arrest."* **What is wrong, and what does it actually mean?**

### 3B · See


![A ladder of past deduction from must have at the top through might well have and unlikely to have down to cannot have at the bottom.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Six levels, about the past — Every rung has to be bought with a piece of evidence, and the price goes up as you climb.*


### 3C · Know

| Certainty | About now | About the past |
|---|---|---|
| **near certain, yes** | *It **must be** two people.* | *There **must have been** two of them.* |
| **probable** | *It**'s likely to be**…* | *It**'s likely to have been**…* |
| **possible** | *It **might / could be**…* | *It **might / could have been**…* |
| **strengthened possible** | *It **might well be**…* | *It **might well have been**…* |
| **improbable** | *It**'s unlikely to be**…* | *It**'s unlikely to have been**…* |
| **near certain, no** | *It **can't be**…* | *It **can't have been**…* |

> **The negative of *must* is *can't*.**
> *mustn't* means *it is forbidden*, which in a clinical note is a very different sentence.

> **Adverbs raise or lower the whole scale**, and only one at a time:
> *certainly · definitely · undoubtedly · doubtless · presumably · plausibly · arguably ·
> conceivably · seemingly · apparently · implausibly*

> ***seemingly* and *apparently* are not about your confidence.** They report somebody else's
> account or an appearance, and in a written reconstruction they are a way of not committing.

> **Why it matters.** A reconstruction that states everything at the same level of certainty is
> useless, because the reader cannot tell which parts to act on.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> There (1) __________ (must / be) at least two people in the bay, because there are two sets
> of gloves. There (2) __________ (can't / be) more than two, because the third pair is still
> sealed. The dressing pack was opened and not used, which (3) __________ (must / mean) that
> somebody started something and stopped. He (4) __________ (might well / be) called away. It
> (5) __________ (be / unlikely) to have been an arrest, because the crash trolley has not been
> opened, and on the balance of probability it (6) __________ (be) a transfer.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one at the certainty level in brackets.

1. There were two people. *(near certain)*
2. He was called away. *(possible, strengthened)*
3. It was not an arrest. *(improbable, not impossible)*
4. The coffee was made before the monitor was switched on. *(near certain, negative of the
   opposite)*
5. Somebody came back for the phone. *(possible, weak)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you describes a photograph the other cannot see — a room, a street,
a kitchen. The other must produce **six deductions at six different certainty levels.**
Then swap.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing mustn't have been and can't have been, with the prohibition reading and the deduction reading of each.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The negative that means something else — The negative that means something else*


**Read both versions. Then say what the wrong one would mean in a hospital note.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. There ______ (must / be) two of them. *(past)*
> 2. There ______ (can't / be) more than two. *(past)*
> 3. He ______ (might well / be) called away. *(past)*
> 4. It ______ unlikely to ______ been an arrest.
> 5. The monitor is on, so somebody ______ (must / be) here recently. *(past)*
> 6. It ______ (can't / be) him — he was in theatre. *(past)*
> 7. She ______ (must / be) in the building. *(now — her coat is here)*
> 8. On the balance of ______ , it was a transfer.

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 22–25. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 85.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — six levels

Your teacher shows one object. Each learner must make a deduction **one level less certain than
the last.** The round ends when somebody reaches *conceivably*.

**Four objects. Four rounds.**

### 4B · The functional core — reading evidence out loud


![Two panels compared: an empty hospital bay with objects in it and a staff movement log with times and names.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The bay, and the movement log — Learner A has the photographs. Learner B has the log. Two minutes are unaccounted for.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the observation** | *There are two sets of…* · *The trolley is at…* |
| **the inference** | *Which points to…* · *That's consistent with…* |
| **the exclusion** | *That rules out…* · *It can't have been…, because…* |
| **the honest gap** | *I can't account for the…* · *We may never establish that.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has photographs of the bay. Learner B has the staff movement log
for the hour. **Find the two minutes that nobody can account for.**

### 4C · The performance — two minutes ⏺

Reconstruct **an event from physical evidence alone**. **Two minutes.** You must include:

> four observations · three inferences at three different certainty levels ·
> one thing you can rule out, with the reason · one thing you cannot explain

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: did any inference get stated more confidently than its
evidence allowed?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use *must have*, *can't have* and one middle form?
> ☐ Is every inference attached to an observation?
> ☐ Did I name the thing I cannot explain?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A review of a triage scoring system. The title is
**"It is a very good instrument and it cannot do this."**
**What do you expect the reviewer to say it cannot do?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**It is a very good instrument and it cannot do this ★★★★☆**

The scoring system in use here is well validated, quick, and better than the judgement of most
people most of the time, which is a higher bar than it sounds.

It takes eleven variables, weights them, and produces a number between 0 and 40. Above 28,
intensive care. Below 12, ward. Between the two, clinical judgement, which is the system politely
admitting that it is a sorting tool and not an oracle.

Here is what it cannot do, and the manual says so on page 4 in a sentence nobody reads.

It cannot distinguish between two patients with the same score. It was never designed to. The
score is a prediction of deterioration over the next six hours and it carries a confidence
interval of about four points in either direction, which means two patients scoring 31 and 31 are
indistinguishable to it, and so, for that matter, are two patients scoring 29 and 33.

This matters exactly once in a while and then it matters enormously. There is one intensive-care
bed. Two patients score 31. The instrument has done its job perfectly and has told you nothing,
and the decision has to be made by a person at four in the morning who will have to write
something down afterwards.

What the manual recommends in that situation is that the decision be made on clinical grounds and
documented. What it does not say — and what three of the four consultants I spoke to said
independently — is that in practice the tie is broken by who arrived first, and that nobody
writes that down.

Four stars. The fifth is withheld not from the instrument but from the page of the manual that
should exist and does not.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many variables does the system use, and what is the range of the score?
2. What are the two thresholds, and what happens between them?
3. **What does the reviewer mean by "the system politely admitting it is a sorting tool"?**
4. What is the confidence interval, and what does that imply about two scores of 31?
5. **Why does the reviewer say two patients scoring 29 and 33 are also indistinguishable?**
6. What does the manual recommend for a tie?
7. **What do three of the four consultants say actually happens?**
8. Why is the fifth star withheld?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> well validated · a higher bar than it sounds · an oracle · a confidence interval ·
> independently

### 5E · The counter-text


![Two triage assessment sheets side by side showing identical total scores with different component variables, arrival times and free-text notes.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Two triage sheets, one bed — Eleven variables, two scores of 31, and a sentence on page 4 that nobody reads.*


**Read the two triage sheets and answer.**

1. What are the two scores, and on how many of the eleven variables do the patients differ?
2. **Which variables are different, and in which direction?**
3. What does page 4 of the manual say, word for word?
4. What is recorded in the free-text box of each sheet?
5. One sheet has a time on it that the other does not. Which, and what time?

### 5F · Two texts

The review says the tie is broken in practice by **who arrived first**.
The two sheets show arrival times **eleven minutes apart**.

**Put those two facts together. What would the record show, and what would it not show?**
Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The adverbs of certainty, and the two that are not

### 6A · Sort — raising, lowering, or reporting?

Three columns.

> apparently · plausibly · doubtless · presumably · certainly · implausibly ·
> conceivably · undoubtedly · seemingly · definitely · arguably

**Two of them belong in the third column and are regularly used as if they belonged in the
first.** Which?

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **All the evidence ______** that there were two people.
2. **There is no ______** that she was in the building at four.
3. **On the balance of ______** it was a transfer.
4. He **might ______ have** been called away.
5. That version does not **______ up** against the movement log.
6. The timeline does not **______ up** when you add the coffee.

### 6C · Three that weaken what they attach to

**seemingly · apparently · arguably**

> *Seemingly* reports an appearance. *Apparently* reports somebody else. *Arguably* reports that
> somebody could argue it — including you.

**Rewrite each sentence without the adverb, and say what is gained and lost.**

1. The patient was seemingly stable at four.
2. Apparently nobody was called.
3. This is arguably the most important variable.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **We may never establish that.**

**Say it twice:** as a closing of an investigation, and as a refusal to close one.
**What changes?**

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Give the certainty level asked for, about the past.

1. The monitor was switched on before the gloves were used. *(probable)*
2. He was called away. *(strengthened possible)*
3. It was an arrest. *(improbable)*
4. She made the coffee. *(weak possible)*
5. Nobody else was here. *(near certain, negative)*
6. Two people were here. *(near certain)*
7. The decision was made on arrival order. *(you could argue it)*
8. The door was wedged by the porter. *(you are reporting what somebody else said)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The h that is not there


![Five past-modal phrases shown written and spoken, with have reduced to a schwa and the h dropped in each.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The h that is not there — After a consonant, the h of have disappears entirely. The spelling never admits it.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 4.3

> must have been → /ˈmʌstəvbɪn/
> can't have been → /ˈkɑːntəvbɪn/
> might well have been → /maɪtˈwelːəvbɪn/
> is unlikely to have been → /ɪzʌnˈlaɪklitəvbɪn/

**The *h* of *have* disappears after a consonant in every one of these.**

### 7B · Say

Say each one. Then say the whole sentence three times, faster:

> *There must have been two of them, and there can't have been more.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *There MUST have been two.* *(somebody has just said one)*
> *There must've been two.* *(and this is simply what the gloves tell you)*

**One of these belongs in a reconstruction and one belongs in an argument about it.** Which?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **six deductions about this room, at six certainty levels.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A reconstruction · 160 words


![A hospital bay drawn in section showing the shelf, the trolley, the bin and the floor, each labelled with what was found there and what it rules out.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*Bay 3, in section — Four levels, and the evidence on three of them agrees. The fourth is the coffee.*


### 8A · Read the model

> Two pairs of gloves are in the bin and a third is sealed on the shelf. There must have been two
> people, and there can't have been three. `[observation first, then two deductions, and each is
> tied to the same object]`
>
> A dressing pack has been opened and not used. That is consistent with somebody starting a
> procedure and stopping, and it is equally consistent with the wrong pack being opened, which is
> commoner. `[the honest part: the same evidence supports two stories, and the writer names the
> likelier one without pretending to have chosen]`
>
> The crash trolley is sealed, so it is unlikely to have been an arrest. `[an exclusion, with its
> physical reason, at the right strength — unlikely, not impossible]`
>
> I cannot account for the coffee. It has been there longer than any of this. `[one unexplained
> thing, stated plainly and not explained away]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The second paragraph gives two readings of one object. **Does that weaken the reconstruction?**
2. *Unlikely*, not *impossible*, for the arrest. **What would be lost by writing *impossible*?**
3. The last paragraph admits a failure. **Why is that the strongest part?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> It appears that there were two staff present and that a procedure was commenced but not
> completed. It seems likely that the patient was transferred. It is possible that staff were
> called away to another emergency. Further information would be required to confirm.

Correct English. **Name three things that are missing**, and say why *appears*, *seems* and
*possible* are all doing the same job here.

### 8C · Plan

> observations, with the objects named → deductions, each tied to an object and graded →
> one exclusion, with its physical reason → one thing you cannot account for

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every inference attached to a named object?
> ☐ Are the certainty levels different from each other?
> ☐ Is there one unexplained thing left unexplained?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the two triage sheets from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# WORK FILE · The Negotiation


![A transcript summary of a negotiation between two consultants over a single intensive-care bed, with each move labelled and the agreed record set out.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Eleven minutes, and what was written down — A negotiation about one bed, reduced to its four moves and its one record.*


### 9A · One bed 🔊 Track 4.4

Two consultants, one intensive-care bed, two patients scoring 31. **Listen to all eleven
minutes.**

1. What does each of them open with, and how long before anybody says a number?
2. **What does one of them offer, and what does the other one ask for in return?**
3. What is actually agreed, and what is written down?

### 9B · The four moves

> stating the position · finding what is behind the other position ·
> making a conditional offer · recording what was agreed

Match each to one phrase.

> *"Mine is 31 and she's twenty-six."* · *"What are you actually worried about tonight?"* ·
> *"If you can hold yours on the ward with one-to-one, I'll take the next bed at eight."* ·
> *"So: mine now, yours at eight, and I'm writing that in both sets of notes."*

### 9C · What a negotiation about a bed is not

This is not a negotiation about who is right. Both scores are 31 and the instrument has told
them nothing.

**Which two of these four sentences would make the conversation worse, and why?**

1. *"The score is the score."*
2. *"Mine arrived first."*
3. *"What does yours look like in four hours if we're wrong?"*
4. *"I've been doing this for eighteen years."*

### 9D · Your turn

In pairs. **Twelve minutes.** One resource, two claims, identical merit.
**Neither of you may use seniority or arrival order as an argument.**
Both must leave with something written.

### 9E · Transfer

**Find one decision this week that was made on an unwritten tiebreak.** Write down what the
tiebreak was and whether anybody would defend it out loud.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of one hour in a hospital bay showing staff movements, objects left behind, two triage assessments and the bed decision.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*One hour in bay 3 — Half the class sees the photographs. Half sees the log. Rebuild the hour by speaking.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class sees the photographs of the bay. Half sees the movement log.
**Rebuild the hour by speaking.** Twelve minutes.

### 10B · Simplify

Explain the triage score to a patient's family who have asked why the other patient got the bed.
**Five sentences only.**

> **Plan:** what the score measures → what it does not → what the two scores were →
> what decided it → what you will write down

### 10C · Compress

The review in Part 5 into **forty words**, keeping the point about page 4.

### 10D · Online

Write **one message** to the clinical governance lead: you have two sheets with identical
scores, the tiebreak was not recorded, and you are proposing one specific change to the form.

### 10E · Your language

How does your language grade certainty? **Does it use modal verbs, adverbs, or verb endings?**
Say *there must have been two of them* and *there might well have been two of them* in both
languages.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Two thirty-ones

One intensive-care bed. Two patients. Both score 31. The instrument has done its job and has
told the department nothing.

- **Arrival order.** Patient A arrived eleven minutes earlier. It is what happens now and nobody
  writes it down.
- **Clinical judgement, documented.** What the manual says. It takes ten minutes that neither
  patient has and produces a sentence somebody has to defend.
- **The sickest-in-four-hours question.** Ask which of the two is more likely to deteriorate
  before the next bed. It is a different question from the score and the score cannot answer it.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of predicted deterioration rates by triage score band, with error bars of four points showing heavy overlap between adjacent bands.](figures/fig_b12_u04_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*What a score of 31 actually predicts — Deterioration within six hours, by score band, with the confidence interval drawn on.*


1. **The two triage sheets** from Part 5E — and the variables where they differ.
2. **The chart** — what a score of 31 actually predicts, with its confidence interval.
3. **The voice.** Dr Herrera, thirty seconds: *"I have made this decision about nine times. Twice
   I have written down the real reason. Both of those times somebody came back to me about it,
   which I am told is the system working, and which I notice has changed what I write."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *The evidence points to ______ . But it rules out nothing, so ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *evidence · consistent · inconsistent · the most likely explanation ·
rule out · account for · on the balance of probability · all the evidence suggests*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think they should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Ask the four-hour question, decide on it, and write the answer down in both sets of notes.
> Arrival order is defensible and nobody defends it, which is the worst combination available:
> it produces a decision that is probably fine and a record that cannot be reviewed. Writing the
> real reason costs the person who writes it, and that cost is the only mechanism by which this
> department will ever find out whether its tiebreaks are any good.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** They asked the four-hour question. It changed the answer: patient B, who
> had arrived second, had a respiratory variable that the score weights lightly and that both
> consultants independently thought mattered more than its weight.
>
> Patient B went to intensive care. Patient A deteriorated at six and went in at eight on the
> next bed, as planned, and was fine.
>
> The reason was written in both sets of notes. It was reviewed in November as part of an audit
> nobody expected, and the respiratory variable's weighting is now being looked at, which is
> either a vindication or a coincidence and will not be known for two years.

**They wrote it down. Somebody read it. And a variable's weighting is being re-examined because
one person spent ten minutes they did not have.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the audit proves.

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **WHAT IS IN THE ROOM** · evidence · trace · residue · position · angle · scene
> **HOW IT FITS** · sequence · timeline · consistent · inconsistent · be consistent with · point to · point towards
> **WHAT YOU DO WITH IT** · reconstruct · witness · piece together · rule out · narrow down · account for · at first glance · on closer inspection
> **THE CONCLUSION** · the most likely explanation · none of it adds up · that explains the rest
> **NEAR CERTAIN** · certainly · definitely · undoubtedly · doubtless · must have · can't have · there is no way
> **IN BETWEEN** · presumably · plausibly · arguably · conceivably · might well have · is unlikely to have · all the evidence suggests · on the balance of probability

Match each word to one place on the map.

> **projection** · **forecast** · **target** · **deadline** · **milestone** · **assumption** ·
> **baseline** · **capacity** · **demand** · **shortfall**

> assumption · demand

### 1C · Meet — three words that describe the same gap

> **variance** · **shortfall** · **demand**

**Variance** is the difference between plan and reality. **Shortfall** is the same difference
with a tone attached. **Demand** is the thing that caused it.

> **f** **It has never been four per cent.**

> **Which, and what is the sentence between them?**

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Finished by then, or still going then

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, is the passing of eleven thousand finished by March or happening in March?
2. In sentence 2, what is the state of affairs in March?
3. Sentence 3 has two future points. **Which is the deadline and which is the event?**
4. Sentence 4 uses *will have had*. **How is that different from *will have*?**
5. Try *"By March we will pass eleven thousand."* **Is it wrong? What does it lose?**

### 3B · See


![A timeline with a future point marked, showing a completed action before it, an ongoing action across it and a duration running up to it.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Finished by then, or still going then — Three future forms, one deadline, and they answer three different questions.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Use it for |
|---|---|---|
| **future continuous** | *will be* + *-ing* | what will be **going on** at a future point |
| **future perfect** | *will have* + past participle | what will be **finished** by a future point |
| **future perfect continuous** | *will have been* + *-ing* | how **long** something will have been going by then |

> **The deadline word is *by*.** *by March*, *by the end of the year*, *by then*.
> *By* means at or before. *Until* means up to and not after, and they are not interchangeable.
> ✓ *We'll have finished by March.* ✗ *We'll have finished until March.*

> ***by the time* takes a present tense**, like all time clauses.
> ✓ *By the time the ward **opens**, the list will have doubled.*
> ✗ *By the time the ward will open…*

> **The future perfect is the tense of plans and audits.** It is how you describe a state of the
> world at a date, which is exactly what a projection is.

> **Watch out.** State verbs still resist the continuous.
> ✓ *By then we will know.* ✗ *By then we will be knowing.*

### 3D · Use — controlled

> By March the list (1) __________ (pass) eleven thousand. By the end of the year the
> department (2) __________ (run) at over ninety per cent capacity, which it
> (3) __________ (do) continuously for about eighteen months by that point. By the time the new
> ward (4) __________ (open), the list (5) __________ (double). This time next year we
> (6) __________ (have) three consecutive years of nine per cent growth, and the model
> (7) __________ (still / assume) four. By 2029, on current trends, we
> (8) __________ (run out) of corridor.

### 3E · Use — guided

Choose the right future form, and say why the others fail.

1. At six o'clock tomorrow I ______ (sit) in a meeting about this.
2. By six o'clock tomorrow I ______ (finish) the paper.
3. By the time you read this, I ______ (leave).
4. In June we ______ (work) on this model for two years.
5. By 2027 the department ______ (know) whether the assumption was right.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe the same future point — *this time in three years* — four times:
once with *will*, once with *will be doing*, once with *will have done*, once with
*will have been doing*. **Then say which was hardest and why.**

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing by and until with a completed future action, showing where each one puts the finishing.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The deadline word, and the one that is not — The deadline word, and the one that is not*


**Read both versions. Then say what the first one commits the writer to that the second does
not.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. By March we ______ (pass) eleven thousand.
> 2. By March we ______ (run) at ninety per cent.
> 3. By the time the ward ______ (open), the list ______ (double).
> 4. In June we ______ (work) on it for two years.
> 5. By then we ______ (know) the answer.
> 6. We ______ (finish) ______ March. *(deadline)*
> 7. At four tomorrow I ______ (present) this to the board.
> 8. By 2029 we ______ (run out) of corridor.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — three years

Your teacher names a date three years out. Each learner says one sentence beginning
*"By then I will have…"* and one beginning *"By then I will be…"*.
**Nobody may use the same verb twice.**

### 4B · The functional core — presenting a projection


![Two panels comparing a modelled growth curve and the actual recorded figures, diverging after the second year.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The modelled world, and the actual one — Learner A has the assumptions. Learner B has six years of actuals. They agreed until 2022.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the projection** | *By March we'll have…* · *On current trends…* |
| **the assumption, out loud** | *This assumes…* · *That number is a working assumption.* |
| **the sensitivity** | *If it's nine rather than four, then…* · *Best case… worst case…* |
| **the honest ending** | *It has never been four per cent.* · *I am planning on a number I do not believe.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the model's assumptions. Learner B has the last six years of
actual figures. **Find the year in which the two stopped agreeing.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Present a **three-year projection**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> the baseline · the projection at your central assumption · the assumption, said out loud ·
> what happens at a different assumption · the point at which the plan breaks

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was every number attached to the assumption that produced
it?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use the future perfect and the future continuous for different jobs?
> ☐ Did I say the assumption out loud, in a sentence of its own?
> ☐ Did I give a second number for a different assumption?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An opinion column. The title is
**"A plan that is refused is not a failure. A plan that is approved and wrong is."**
**What do you expect the argument to be?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**A plan that is refused is not a failure. A plan that is approved and wrong is**

Every public body I have worked in has had the same quiet arrangement, and nobody has ever
written it down because writing it down would end it.

A department produces a three-year plan. The plan contains an assumption about demand. The
assumption is chosen not because anybody believes it but because it is the number that produces a
plan which can be approved. If you put the real number in, the plan asks for money that does not
exist, and a plan that asks for money that does not exist is returned for revision, which costs
four months.

So the department puts in four per cent. Demand rises nine. In year two somebody notices, and
what they notice is not that the assumption was wrong — everybody knew the assumption was wrong —
but that the department has now been running on an approved plan for two years and has no
document anywhere that says what it actually needs.

That is the real cost, and it is not financial. It is that at the end of three years there will
be no record of the gap. There will be a plan that was met, a service that is failing, and no
piece of paper connecting the two.

I am not arguing that departments should submit plans that will be refused. I am arguing that
they should submit the four per cent plan **and** a one-page annex saying what the nine per cent
world looks like, and that they should do this every year whether anybody reads it or not,
because in four years somebody will ask when this started and somebody ought to be able to
answer.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What is the "quiet arrangement" the writer describes?
2. **Why is the assumption chosen, according to the writer?**
3. What happens to a plan that asks for money that does not exist, and what does that cost?
4. What does somebody notice in year two, and what do they **not** notice?
5. **What does the writer say the real cost is?**
6. What are the three things that will exist at the end of three years?
7. What is the writer's actual proposal?
8. **Why does the writer say it should be done "whether anybody reads it or not"?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> a quiet arrangement · returned for revision · running on an approved plan · an annex ·
> connecting the two

### 5E · The counter-text


![A three-year planning model showing baseline, assumed growth and projected totals alongside the recorded actual growth rates for the same years.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The three-year model, and the actual figures — One cell carries a footnote. It is the only place the real number appears.*


**Read the three-year model and the actual figures beside it, and answer.**

1. What is the baseline, and what growth rate does the model assume?
2. **What are the actual rates for the last three years, and what is the cumulative variance?**
3. What does the model project for year three, and what does nine per cent project?
4. When was the assumption last reviewed, and by whom?
5. One cell has a footnote. Which, and what does it say?

### 5F · Two texts

The column says a department ends up with **no document saying what it needs**.
The model has a cell with a footnote giving the real figure.

**Put those two facts together. Does the footnote count?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Putting things ahead of you

### 6A · Sort — a point, a period, or a direction?

Three columns.

> meanwhile · thereafter · progressively · ultimately · imminently · eventually ·
> cumulatively · annually · henceforth · by · shortly

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **By ______** the list will have passed eleven thousand.
2. **Within the ______** the new ward will be open or it will be cancelled.
3. **Over the ______** , demand has risen twenty-nine per cent.
4. **Year ______ year** , the model has been out by five points.
5. **At this ______** , we will run out of corridor in 2029.
6. They have failed to **see ______** a single one of the four milestones.

### 6C · Three that look like timing and are not

**ultimately · eventually · henceforth**

> *Ultimately* is about the end state, not the timing. *Eventually* is about the timing and
> refuses to give one. *Henceforth* is about a rule starting, and is nearly always too formal.

**Rewrite each sentence with a date in it, and say what is gained.**

1. Ultimately the ward will open.
2. The list will eventually come down.
3. Henceforth the assumption will be reviewed annually.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **This time next year we will have…**

**Complete it three times:** once for something certain, once for something planned,
once for something you do not believe.

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Future simple, continuous, perfect or perfect continuous.

1. By the time it opens, the list ______ (double).
2. By next March we ______ (reach) ninety per cent. *(an event completed)*
3. By four tomorrow I ______ (present) this.
4. At four tomorrow I ______ (present) this.
5. By June we ______ (work) on it for two years.
6. Next March we ______ (run) at ninety per cent. *(a state of affairs)*
7. By then we ______ (know). *(a state)*
8. By 2029 we ______ (run out) of corridor.

### Four weak forms in a row


![Five future-perfect phrases with the stressed syllables drawn as large dots, showing long chains of unstressed function words.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Four weak forms in a row — In we will have been running, four words carry no stress at all and one does.*

> we will have been running → /wiːləvbɪnˈrʌnɪŋ/
> by the time it opens → /baɪðəˈtaɪmɪtˈəʊpənz/

**In *we will have been running*, four words carry no stress at all and one does.**

Say each one. Then say the whole sentence three times, faster:

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A three-year projection · 160 words


![A hospital wing drawn in section showing the plant room, two finished floors, the ward that has not opened and the corridor beneath it.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*The new wing, in section — Four floors, and the one that was due last year is the one the plan depends on.*


### 8A · Read the model

> The baseline is 7,420 on the list at 31 March. The model assumes four per cent annual growth,
> which gives 8,350 by year three. `[the baseline, the assumption and the result, in that order
> and in one breath]`
>
> That four per cent is a working assumption and has not been reviewed since 2019. Actual growth
> has been 9.1 and 8.8 per cent in the last two years. At nine per cent the year-three figure is
> 9,610. `[the assumption named as an assumption, then the actual figures, then the second
> number]`
>
> By the time the new ward opens in Q2 of year two, the list will have passed nine thousand on
> the real figures and 8,000 on the modelled ones. The ward adds capacity for 600.
> `[the point at which the two worlds separate, and the thing that was supposed to fix it]`
>
> I am presenting the four per cent plan because that is the number we are planning on. I would
> like the nine per cent annex minuted. `[what the writer is doing, and what they are asking for,
> separately]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The assumption is named in its own sentence. **Why not in a footnote?**
2. The last paragraph separates what the writer is submitting from what they are asking for.
   **Why does that distinction matter?**
3. There is no adjective anywhere in this piece. **Is that a style choice or an argument?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The department faces significant challenges in the coming period. Demand is expected to
> continue to grow and capacity will need to be reviewed. We remain committed to meeting our
> targets and will continue to monitor the situation closely.

Correct English, and not one number. **Name three numbers it should contain**, and say what
*monitor the situation closely* is standing in for.

### 8C · Plan

> baseline → central assumption, named as an assumption → the result → the actual figures →
> the second result → the point where the two separate → what you are asking for

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the assumption in a sentence of its own?
> ☐ Are there two projections, from two assumptions?
> ☐ Is there a named date at which the two worlds separate?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the model and the actual figures from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# STUDY FILE · Feedback and Revision


![A summary of supervisor feedback across four drafts, classified into four kinds of comment with counts for each round.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four rounds of comments on one proposal — Twenty-nine comments. One of them changed the project, and it is in round two.*


### 9A · Four rounds of comments 🔊 Track 5.4

Matías's project proposal has been through four drafts. **Listen to his supervisor's feedback on
each.**

1. What kind of comment dominates round one, and what kind dominates round four?
2. **Which round contains the comment that actually changed the project?**
3. One comment is repeated in all four rounds. Which, and what does that tell you?

### 9B · Four kinds of comment

> *this is wrong* · *this is unclear* · *this is not what you set out to do* ·
> *this is fine and here is what I would do instead*

Match each to one phrase.

> *"The 2019 figure is 7,420, not 7,240."* ·
> *"I cannot tell whether this sentence is your claim or theirs."* ·
> *"Your question was about capacity and this is about money."* ·
> *"This works. I would still put the assumption first."*

### 9C · The comment you should act on last

**Which of the four kinds is it safe to leave until the final draft, and why?**
And **which one, if you leave it, makes all the other work worthless?**

### 9D · Your turn

In pairs. **Twelve minutes.** Swap the 160 words you wrote in Part 8.
**Give one comment of each of the four kinds, in writing.** Then revise, and say which comment
you acted on first.

### 9E · Transfer

**Find one piece of feedback you have received and not acted on.** Write down which of the four
kinds it was, and why you left it.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Plan honestly, or plan approvably

The three-year plan is due in six weeks. The model assumes four per cent. Demand has risen nine
per cent for two years.

- **Submit at four per cent.** It will be approved. The department will be running on a plan
  that nobody in it believes, for the third year.
- **Submit at nine per cent.** It will be returned for revision, which costs four months, and
  the department will spend those four months on the current plan anyway.
- **Submit at four with a nine per cent annex.** It will be approved and the annex will probably
  not be read, and it will exist.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart of projected waiting-list size at four per cent growth against the actual recorded figures, with the gap widening each year.](figures/fig_b12_u05_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Modelled demand, and actual demand — The two lines separate in the second year and never come back.*


1. **The model and the actuals** from Part 5E — and the footnote on the growth cell.
2. **The chart** — modelled and actual demand, and the point at which they separate.
3. **The voice.** The finance lead, thirty seconds: *"I have submitted the four per cent plan for
   six years. I have never believed it and I have never been criticised for it. If I submit nine
   I will be told to revise it, and when I resubmit at four, which I will, the only thing that
   will have changed is that it will be August."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *By year three we will have ______ , which assumes ______ . If instead ______ , then ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *projection · forecast · target · assumption · baseline · variance ·
capacity · demand · shortfall · on current trends · best case · worst case*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think they should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Submit at four with the annex, and ask for the annex to be minuted rather than attached.
> An attachment can be lost and a minute cannot. The column is right that the real damage is the
> absence of a record, and the cheapest possible record is one sentence in the minutes of a
> meeting that is going to happen anyway. It costs nothing, it will change nothing this year, and
> in four years it will be the only evidence that anybody knew.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The plan went in at four per cent with a one-page annex. The finance lead
> asked for the annex to be minuted and it was, in eleven words.
>
> The plan was approved in April. Demand in that year rose 8.4 per cent. The annex was not
> discussed at any meeting.
>
> In the third year the regional office began a review of why four hospitals had missed the same
> target. One of its first requests was for any documentation showing that departments had
> identified the risk in advance. Three hospitals had nothing. This one had eleven words in a
> minute, and the review's final report quotes them.

**Nobody read the annex. The eleven words in the minute did the whole job, three years later.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the other three hospitals should do now.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Things done to you, and things you arrange

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, who did the referring? **Is it named?**
2. Sentence 2 is a modal passive. **What is the full shape?**
3. Sentence 3 is a perfect passive with *should*. **What is it criticising?**
4. In sentence 4, who is doing the procedure?
   **And who is the subject of the sentence?**
5. Try *"She's doing it on Thursday."* **How does that change who she is?**

### 3B · See


![Two parallel sentences compared, one a passive with the patient as the affected party and one a causative with the patient as the subject.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Done to you, and arranged by you — The same Thursday. The difference is which word the sentence starts with.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **modal passive** | *modal* + *be* + pp | *It **can be done** under local.* |
| **perfect passive** | *have* + *been* + pp | *She **has been referred**.* |
| **modal perfect passive** | *modal* + *have been* + pp | *She **should have been seen** in March.* |
| **causative: have** | *have* + object + pp | *She**'s having it done** on Thursday.* |
| **causative: get** | *get* + object + pp | *I **got my notes sent** to the clinic.* |

> **The causative keeps the patient as the subject** while making it clear that somebody else is
> doing the work. *She is having it done* is not the same as *she is doing it*, and it is not
> the same as *it is being done to her*: it says she arranged it, or that it is happening to her
> as part of her plan.

> ***get* is the informal causative** and carries a faint sense of effort or arrangement:
> *I got it sorted.* *Have* is neutral.

> **The verbs that take an object and a to-infinitive:** *cause, allow, permit, require, oblige,
> entitle, get.* The verbs that take an object and a bare infinitive: *make, let*.
> ✓ *They **made her wait**.* ✓ *They **required her to wait**.* ✗ *They made her to wait.*

> **In the passive, *make* takes *to* again.** ✓ *She **was made to wait**.*

### 3D · Use — controlled

> The patient (1) __________ (refer) by her own doctor in March. She
> (2) __________ (should / see) within eight weeks and was not. The procedure
> (3) __________ (can / do) under local, so she will be awake. She
> (4) __________ (have) it done on Thursday. Her daughter (5) __________ (not / allow) to
> interpret, because the policy (6) __________ (require) a registered interpreter, and she
> (7) __________ (make) to wait four hours for the telephone service on two previous occasions.
> Nothing (8) __________ (explain) to her in a language she speaks.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one. **Keep the patient as the subject.**

1. A consultant will see her. *(passive)*
2. Somebody should have told her in March. *(modal perfect passive)*
3. The hospital is arranging her transport. *(causative)*
4. The policy does not permit her daughter to interpret. *(passive, patient as subject)*
5. They forced her to wait four hours. *(passive)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Describe a service you have used **twice**: once with the institution as the
subject, once with yourself. **Three minutes each.** Then say which version you would send as a
complaint, and which as a thank-you.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing a causative with the past participle and a sentence without it, showing who performs the action in each.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The causative that turns into a confession — The causative that turns into a confession*


**Read both versions. Then say what the first one accidentally says about the patient.**

> 1. She ______ (refer) by her own doctor.
> 2. She ______ (should / see) in March.
> 3. It ______ (can / do) under local.
> 4. She ______ (have) it done on Thursday.

> 6. She ______ (make) to wait four hours.

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A feature article. The title is
**"The four-hour queue exists to protect her, and she has never used it."**
**What do you expect the argument to be?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**The four-hour queue exists to protect her, and she has never used it**

The policy is correct. I want to establish that before anything else, because the piece that
writes itself here is the one about heartless bureaucracy, and that piece would be wrong.

Family members should not interpret in clinical settings. The reasons are not procedural. A
daughter will soften a prognosis. A son will omit a question about alcohol. A sixteen-year-old
will be asked to say the word *terminal* to her own mother, and will be in a room for the rest of
her life afterwards. Every hospital that has studied this has found the same thing, and the
policy that follows from it is the right policy.

The telephone interpreting service that the policy requires has a median wait of four hours and
eleven minutes.

So here is what actually happens, and it happens in every hospital I have visited in three
countries. The clinician makes a judgement. For anything serious, they wait, and the four hours
are real and the clinic runs over and other patients are seen late and the policy works. For
anything routine — a consent for a minor procedure, a discharge explanation, a follow-up date —
they use the daughter, and they do not write down that they used the daughter, because writing it
down is a breach.

The policy therefore achieves two things. It protects patients in serious conversations, which is
what it was for. And it produces a permanent, undocumented, unmeasurable practice in routine
ones, which is the opposite of what a policy is for.

The queue is not a bureaucratic failure attached to a good policy. The queue **is** the policy,
in the sense that a rule that cannot be followed is a rule about what gets written down.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What does the writer establish first, and why?
2. **What three reasons does the writer give for the policy?**
3. What is the median wait for the telephone service?
4. What do clinicians do for serious conversations, and what does that cost?
5. **What do they do for routine ones, and why is it not recorded?**
6. What are the two things the writer says the policy achieves?
7. **What does the writer mean by "the queue is the policy"?**
8. Why does the writer say the obvious article would be wrong?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> the piece that writes itself · soften a prognosis · a median wait · the clinic runs over ·
> a breach

### 5E · The counter-text


![A hospital interpreting policy with its prohibition and exceptions, alongside the telephone service performance figures including median wait and abandonment rate.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The interpreting policy, and the service figures — A correct policy, and the queue that makes it a rule about what gets written down.*


**Read the interpreting policy and the service's own figures, and answer.**

1. What does the policy forbid, and what two exceptions does it list?
2. **What is the median wait, and what is the ninetieth-percentile wait?**
3. How many requests were made last year, and how many were abandoned?
4. What does the policy say must be recorded when a family member is used?
5. One clause defines an emergency. What does it say, and what does it not cover?

### 5F · Two texts

The feature says routine use of family members is **undocumented**.
The policy requires every such use to be **recorded on the clinical record**.

**Put those two facts together. What does the abandonment figure actually measure?**
Three sentences.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The word that moves the stress


![Five short sentences with the stressed syllables drawn as large dots, contrasting causatives with ordinary active sentences.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The word that moves the stress — In a causative the participle takes the stress, and that is most of what tells a listener which one they heard.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 6.3

> She's having it **DONE**. — somebody else does it
> She's **DO**ing it. — she does it
> I had my notes **SENT**. — I arranged it
> I **SENT** my notes. — I did it

**The participle takes the stress in a causative, and that is most of what tells a listener
which one they are hearing.**

### 7B · Say

Say all four. Then say the sentence three times, faster:

> *She's having it done on Thursday and she's having the notes sent on Monday.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *She had her hair cut.* *(a hairdresser)*
> *She had her hair cut.* *(said with stress on HAD — somebody made her)*

**English uses the same words for an arrangement and a misfortune.** Which one is which, here?

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **three things you have had done and three things you did.**
Three times, faster, with the stress in the right place.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A procedure, from the patient's side · 160 words


![A hospital drawn in section showing the ward, the lift, the theatre corridor and day theatre, with the handover at each level labelled.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*The route from the ward to theatre, in section — Four levels, four handovers, and the patient is the subject of none of them.*


### 8A · Read the model

> You will be collected from the ward at about eleven, by a porter, on a trolley, and you will
> not be told which of the three theatres you are going to. `[everything in the passive, the
> patient as subject, and one thing stated as a known absence]`
>
> It is done under local, which means you will be awake and you will feel pressure and not pain,
> and nobody will warn you about the pressure. `[what the words actually mean, in the patient's
> body]`
>
> You can have a relative with you until the door and not past it. You will have to ask for this.
> `[one entitlement and one causative, and the entitlement comes with a condition]`
>
> You will be sent home the same day with a letter you cannot read, because it is written to your
> own doctor. Ask for the second paragraph to be explained before you leave.
> `[the discharge, and one piece of advice that could only come from somebody who has watched
> this]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. Every sentence has *you* as the subject. **What does that do that a procedure leaflet does
   not?**
2. Three sentences say what you will **not** be told. **Is that hostile to the hospital?**
3. The last sentence is an instruction to the reader. **Why end there?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The procedure is performed under local anaesthetic and takes approximately twenty minutes.
> Patients are usually discharged the same day. A discharge summary will be sent to your GP.
> Please contact the department if you have any questions.

Correct English, and the patient appears twice as an object and never as a person.
**Rewrite the first sentence with the patient as the subject**, and name one thing the leaflet
does not say.

### 8C · Plan

> what will be done to you, and by whom → what the technical words mean in your body →
> one thing you have to arrange or ask for → what happens afterwards, and the one question to ask

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the patient the subject of every sentence?
> ☐ Is there one causative and one modal passive?
> ☐ Is there one thing the reader is told to ask for?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the policy from Part 5E beside it.

# CULTURE FILE · The Misunderstanding

> *the clinician speaks to the interpreter instead of the patient* ·
> *the interpreter summarises rather than interprets* ·
> *the patient answers the interpreter rather than the clinician* ·
> *a technical word has no equivalent and is left in English*

notice?**

> *"I'm going to speak to you, and she will say it in your language."* ·
> *"Please interpret everything she said, including that."* ·
> *"Can you tell me what word you used for that?"* ·
> *"I'll say that again more simply."*

> setting up the triangle · asking for completeness · checking a term ·
> repairing your own English

### 9D · Your turn

In threes. **Twelve minutes.** One clinician, one patient, one interpreter — and the
"interpreter" may only use words of two syllables or fewer.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The daughter in the corridor

A patient is being consented for a minor procedure. Her daughter, who is forty-one, is in the
corridor. The policy requires a registered interpreter. The telephone service has a median wait
of four hours and eleven minutes.

- **Follow the policy.** Wait. The clinic runs over by four hours, eleven other patients are
  seen late, and three of them will not be seen today.
- **Use the daughter and record it.** The policy permits this in an emergency only, so recording
  it is recording a breach, which goes to the governance committee and names the clinician.
- **Use the daughter and do not record it.** What happens in practice, everywhere, and the reason
  nobody knows how often.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of interpreting requests and abandonment rate by time of day, with both peaking in the mid-morning clinic period.](figures/fig_b12_u06_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Requests, waits and abandonments, by time of day — Three thousand one hundred and forty requests, and the queue is worst when the clinics are.*


1. **The policy and the service figures** from Part 5E — and the emergency clause.
2. **The chart** — requests, waits and abandonments, by time of day.
3. **The voice.** The patient, through her daughter, thirty seconds: *"I would like my daughter
   to do it. I am aware that she will make it sound better than it is. I have thought about that
   and I would still like my daughter to do it, and nobody has asked me this before, which is the
   part I mind."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *If the policy is followed, ______ . If it is not, ______ . What is not recorded is ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *consent · procedure · interpreter · carer · be allowed to · be required
to · on behalf of · written consent · nobody explained any of it*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the clinician should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Use the daughter, record it as a breach, and write one line saying why. The third option is the
> one everybody takes and it is the only one that guarantees nothing will ever change, because an
> unmeasured practice cannot be argued about. A breach that appears eleven times in a quarter,
> each with a four-hour wait beside it, is the only document that will ever get the service
> funded properly. It costs the clinician something. That is what it is for.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** Dr Benítez recorded it as a breach, with one line: *telephone service wait
> 4 h 20 min, routine consent, patient's explicit request, daughter aged 41.*
>
> The governance committee reviewed it in six weeks and recorded no concern. Over the following
> quarter, eleven more breaches were recorded with similar lines, nine of them by people who had
> seen his.
>
> The service's contract was renegotiated in the following year with a two-hour target for
> routine requests. The business case quotes the twelve breach records, which are the only
> documentation of the problem that has ever existed in this hospital.

**He took the cost. Nine people copied him. And twelve one-line records did what four years of
complaints had not.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should happen to the policy now.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The same verb, two different sentences

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, what did she stop? In sentence 2?
2. In sentence 3, which happened first — the remembering or the checking? In sentence 4?
3. **Is there a pattern across the two pairs?** Look at which form points backwards.
4. Try *try doing* and *try to do*. **Which one is an experiment and which is an effort?**
5. Try *"I regret to tell you"* and *"I regret telling you."* **Which one is bad news for the
   listener?**

### 3B · See


![Two parallel sentences compared, one with stop plus -ing ending an activity and one with stop plus to naming a purpose.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Backwards, and forwards — The -ing form points at something already real. The to form points at a purpose.*


### 3C · Know

| Verb | + *-ing* | + *to* |
|---|---|---|
| **stop** | stop the activity · *I stopped **thinking**.* | stop in order to do · *I stopped **to think**.* |
| **remember** | the memory of doing it · *I remember **checking**.* | the duty, then the act · *I remembered **to check**.* |
| **forget** | *I'll never forget **seeing** it.* | *I forgot **to check**.* |
| **try** | an experiment · *Try **changing** the time.* | an effort · *Try **to change** it.* |
| **go on** | continue the same thing · *He went on **talking**.* | move to a new thing · *He went on **to say** that…* |
| **regret** | about the past · *I regret **saying** it.* | about now · *I regret **to say** that…* |
| **mean** | involve · *It means **leaving** at four.* | intend · *I meant **to leave** at four.* |
| **need** | passive meaning · *It needs **fixing**.* | *I need **to fix** it.* |

> **The pattern, as far as there is one.** The ***-ing* form points backwards or inwards**: to
> something already happening, or already remembered. The ***to* form points forwards**: to a
> purpose, an intention or a duty.

> **It is not a reliable rule.** It covers *stop*, *remember*, *forget*, *go on* and *regret*
> well, *try* and *mean* adequately, and *need* not at all. The eight verbs have to be learned.

> **Why it matters.** *I stopped checking* and *I stopped to check* are opposite accounts of the
> same night, and a hospital record contains both sentences.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> She remembers (1) __________ (check) the chart at about two. She does not remember
> (2) __________ (check) it again after four, and she thinks she forgot
> (3) __________ (do) it. At some point she stopped (4) __________ (think) about whether she
> was tired, which she says is different from (5) __________ (stop / notice). She tried
> (6) __________ (drink) coffee, which did not work, and then she tried
> (7) __________ (stay) on her feet, which did. The rota needs (8) __________ (change), and
> everybody has meant (9) __________ (raise) it for two years.

### 3E · Use — guided

Write both versions, and say what each means.

1. stop + smoke
2. remember + lock the door
3. try + ask somebody else
4. go on + explain
5. regret + tell him

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Tell the story of one shift, one day or one journey, using **six** of the eight
verbs in Part 3C. Your partner's job is to catch any form that produces the wrong meaning.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing I did not remember to check and I do not remember checking, showing what each one claims about the past.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The two sentences a nursing note can contain — The two sentences a nursing note can contain*


**Read both versions. Then say which of the two you would want in a nursing note, and why.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. I stopped ______ (think) about it. *(the thinking ended)*
> 2. I stopped ______ (think) about it. *(I paused in order to)*
> 3. I remember ______ (check) it. *(the memory)*
> 4. I remembered ______ (check) it. *(the duty)*
> 5. Try ______ (change) the time. *(an experiment)*
> 6. He went on ______ (talk) for an hour. *(the same thing)*
> 7. He went on ______ (say) that it was unsafe. *(a new thing)*
> 8. The rota needs ______ (change).

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An interview transcript. The title is
**"I didn't choose the eleventh one. I just didn't stop."**
**What do you expect the difference to be?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**"I didn't choose the eleventh one. I just didn't stop"**
*Interview, night nurse, eleven consecutive nights*

**Why eleven?**
It wasn't eleven at the start. It was four, and then somebody was off, and then I picked up two
because the money is a third more at night and I have a daughter at university in Santiago.

**At what point did it become a decision?**
That is the question, and I have thought about it a lot since, and the honest answer is that it
never did. There is no night on which I decided to do eleven. Around night six I stopped thinking
about it. Not stopped caring — stopped thinking. It went from being a thing I was doing to being
the shape of the week.

**Did anybody say anything?**
Yes. On night eight somebody asked if I was all right and I said I was fine, and I remember
saying it and I remember meaning it.

**And on the eleventh?**
I remember checking a drug chart at about two. I do not remember checking it again, and I
believe I did, and I cannot tell you that I did.

Nothing happened. I want to be clear that nothing happened. That is not a defence, it is the
thing that worries me, because if something had happened there would be a form and a review and
the rota would have been looked at, and because nothing happened there is nothing except this
conversation.

**What would you say to somebody on night six?**
I would say: you are not going to notice night seven. Nobody notices night seven. Get somebody
else to count for you, because the thing that goes first is not your hands and it is not your
judgement, it is your ability to be the person who is counting.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How did eleven nights come about?
2. **What does she say happened around night six, and what distinction does she draw?**
3. What happened on night eight?
4. **What is the difference between what she remembers at two and what she remembers after?**
5. Why does she say "nothing happened" is not a defence?
6. What would have existed if something had happened?
7. What is her advice to somebody on night six?
8. **What does she say goes first?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> picked up two · the shape of the week · a defence · looked at · the person who is counting

### 5E · The counter-text


![A hospital night rota policy showing consecutive-shift limits, approval requirements and rest periods, alongside the actual rota with handwritten amendments.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The rota policy, and the rota — A printed policy, a printed rota, and a clause added to the rota in biro.*


**Read the rota policy and the actual rota, and answer.**

1. What is the maximum number of consecutive nights the policy permits?
2. **What does the policy say about voluntary additional shifts, and who has to approve them?**
3. How many of the eleven nights were approved, and by whom?
4. What does the policy say about the rest period, and what rest did she actually have?
5. One clause has been added by hand to the printed rota. What does it say?

### 5F · Two texts

The interview says **nothing happened**.
The incident log for those eleven nights shows **two near misses, neither involving her**.

**Put those two facts together. What does the log actually tell you?** Three sentences.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Stress as the disambiguator


![Five sentence pairs with the stressed syllables drawn as large dots, distinguishing gerund and infinitive readings.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Stress as the disambiguator — Two letters in writing. In speech it is mostly rhythm that tells the two apart.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 7.3

> I stopped **THINK**ing. — the thinking ended
> I **STOPPED** to think. — I paused, in order to
> I remember **CHECK**ing. — the memory
> I re**MEM**bered to check. — the duty

**The two forms are distinguished in writing by two letters and in speech mostly by rhythm.**

### 7B · Say

Say all four. Then say the pair three times, faster, keeping them distinct:

> *I stopped thinking about it, and then I stopped to think about it.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *I didn't remember to check.* *(I failed in a duty)*
> *I don't remember checking.* *(I may have done it)*

**One of these is a confession and one is a gap.** Which would you rather write in a note?

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### Something I tried to change · 160 words


![A diagram of eleven consecutive night shifts drawn as four stages, from a planned block through covering absence to a run with no decision point.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*Eleven nights, in section — Four stages, and the one that matters is the one with no decision in it.*


### 8A · Read the model

> I tried to stop completely in January and I lasted nine days. In March I tried cutting down to
> three a day, which worked for about seven weeks. `[the effort and the experiment, both named,
> and both with a number attached]`
>
> What I eventually noticed is that it was never about the cigarette. It was about going outside.
> I had been going outside eleven times a day for six years and I had stopped noticing that I did
> it. `[the trigger, and it is a situation and not a weakness]`
>
> I now go outside eleven times a day and stand in the same place. That is the whole of the
> change and it is not a triumph; it is a substitution and it may not hold.
> `[what actually happened, named accurately, and the writer refuses the ending the reader wants]`
>
> I did not use willpower. I do not think I have any. `[one sentence that disposes of the
> category]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. Both forms of *try* appear, with different results. **What does that let the writer show?**
2. The trigger is *going outside*, not the cigarette. **Why is that a better explanation?**
3. The last two sentences dismiss willpower. **Is that an excuse?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> I decided to give up and it was very difficult at first. There were times when I wanted to give
> in but I stayed strong and reminded myself why I was doing it. It just takes determination and
> the right mindset. Anyone can do it if they really want to.

Correct English, and useless to anybody trying. **Name three things it does not contain**,
and say what *the right mindset* is standing in for.

### 8C · Plan

> what you tried, in both forms, with numbers → the trigger, as a situation →
> what actually happened → one sentence that refuses the tidy ending

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Are both forms of at least one verb used, with different meanings?
> ☐ Is the trigger a situation rather than a character flaw?
> ☐ Does the ending refuse to be a triumph?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the rota from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Night twelve

Carla has worked eleven consecutive nights. She chose to, she needs the money, the rota permits
it, and a colleague has said she thinks it is unsafe. Night twelve is on the rota.

- **Let it run.** She is an adult, the policy permits twelve, nothing has happened, and she needs
  the money more than anybody else needs her to stop.
- **Stop her.** Take her off night twelve. She loses a third of a night's pay, somebody has to
  cover, and she will be the nurse who was taken off.
- **Change the policy.** Cap consecutive nights at seven for everybody. It costs about four
  shifts a month in agency cover and it takes the decision away from the person least able to
  make it at four in the morning.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart of error rate against number of consecutive night shifts, rising sharply after the seventh night in both the literature and local data.](figures/fig_b12_u07_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Error rate by consecutive night — The published literature, and this hospital's own figures, on the same axes.*


1. **The policy and the rota** from Part 5E — and the clause added by hand.
2. **The chart** — error rates by consecutive night, from the literature and from this hospital.
3. **The voice.** Carla, thirty seconds: *"If you ask me now, I will tell you I am fine, and the
   reason I will tell you that is that I am the person being asked. I said it on night eight and
   I meant it. I would quite like somebody to stop asking me and decide."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *She stopped ______ , which is not the same as stopping ______ . So ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *habit · dependence · trigger · relapse · willpower · focus · attention ·
without noticing · the first week*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the hospital should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Change the policy to seven and pay for the agency cover. Carla is right that she is the worst
> possible person to ask, and so is everybody else at four in the morning on their eleventh
> night; that is not a fact about her character, it is what the research says about everybody's.
> A cap is the only version of this that does not require one tired person to be heroic about
> their own tiredness, and it costs four shifts a month, which is a number the hospital can
> actually decide about.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The cap was proposed at seven and agreed at nine, because seven would have
> required more agency cover than the budget had.
>
> Carla's income fell by about eleven per cent. She has said twice, publicly, that she supports
> the cap, and once, privately, that she has been looking at agency work herself because the
> agency rate for a single night is higher than the hospital's.
>
> Consecutive-night incidents in the following year fell from nine to four. Nobody can say
> whether that is the cap, because nine and four are small numbers and the department also got a
> new consultant in March.

**The cap worked, probably, and it cost one person eleven per cent of her income, definitely.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should be done about the agency rate.

**By the end of this unit you can explain a category or a statistic to somebody outside the
field.**

|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | articles and determiners, advanced · generic reference · 23 words for categories and data · 22 determiners and pointers · /ðə/ /ðiː/ /ə/ /eɪ/ |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A category explained to somebody who does not work here |

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Three ways of saying everybody

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, how many patients?
2. In sentence 3, is there a particular patient?
3. **Sentences 1 and 3 both make a general claim. What is the difference in feel?**
4. Sentence 4 uses *the* with a singular. **Can that ever be generic? Try: *The patient is the
   last person consulted.***
5. Try *"The patients wait too long."* **Who exactly is waiting?**

### 3B · See


![Four versions of one sentence with brackets marking the article in each and what it points at.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Four ways of saying everybody, and one of them is not — The fourth is never generic. It always means those ones, and the reader has to know which.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Means |
|---|---|---|
| **bare plural** | *Patients wait too long.* | all of them, everywhere, as a class |
| **a / an + singular** | *A patient waits 41 days.* | any one, as a representative type |
| **the + singular** | *The patient is the last to be consulted.* | the category as an institution — formal |
| **the + plural** | *The patients waited 41 days.* | **a specific group**, already identified |

> **The trap is the fourth row.** *The patients* is never generic in ordinary English. It always
> means *those patients*, and if the reader does not already know which ones, the sentence is
> broken.

> **Determiners that narrow further:** *certain patients* (some, unspecified) ·
> *given patients* (specified, in a formula) · *such patients* (of the kind just described) ·
> *the respective departments* (each its own) · *the former / the latter* (of two just named).

> **The phrases that carry a definition:** *by definition · for the purposes of · in principle ·
> in practice · as a whole · in the main*.

> **Why it matters.** A report that says *the patients* without having said which ones is a
> report that four departments will read four ways, and all four will be reading in good faith.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ patients wait too long. That is a general claim and nobody disputes it.
> (2) __________ patients in this report waited 41 days on average — but
> (3) __________ report does not say which patients, and (4) __________ four departments
> reading it each have (5) __________ working definition of their own. For
> (6) __________ purposes of finance, a day case is an admission. In clinical coding it is not.
> (7) __________ latter definition produces a figure four hundred lower, and
> (8) __________ ambiguity has never been raised because nobody owns the report.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one so it says what the writer means.

1. *The patients are waiting longer.* *(meaning: all patients, everywhere)*
2. *Patients waited 41 days.* *(meaning: the 1,340 in this report)*
3. *A patient is entitled to an interpreter.* *(meaning: every patient, as a rule)*
4. *The patient is not consulted.* *(meaning: the category, in a formal sentence)*
5. *Such patients are excluded.* *(meaning: the ones described in the previous sentence)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Take any number from your own life — a price, a grade, a waiting time. State it
four times: about everybody, about a type, about a specific group, and about the category as an
institution. **Your partner says which is which.**

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing a sentence with an undefined definite plural and the same sentence with the population named.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The sentence that produces four reports — The sentence that produces four reports*


**Read both versions. Then say how many different reports the first sentence produces.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ patients wait too long. *(all, everywhere)*
> 2. ______ patients in this report waited 41 days. *(this group)*
> 3. ______ patient waits 41 days. *(a representative type)*
> 4. ______ patient is the last to be consulted. *(the category, formal)*
> 5. ______ patients are excluded. *(of the kind just described)*
> 6. ______ departments each produced their own figure. *(each its own)*
> 7. Of the two definitions, ______ produces a lower figure. *(the second)*
> 8. ______ the purposes of this report, a day case is an admission.

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 46–49. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 173.

![Two panels compared: two overlapping populations of patients, one including day cases and one excluding them.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

| | |
|---|---|
| **the definition** | *By definition…* · *For the purposes of this…* |
| **the boundary** | *What falls under it is…* · *What it excludes is…* |
