## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An archive document: the minutes of the meeting at which a definition was set, in 2011.
The title is **"Item 7: what counts as an admission."**
**What do you expect the meeting to have decided?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**MINUTES OF THE INFORMATION GROUP — 9 NOVEMBER 2011**
**Item 7: what counts as an admission**

**7.1** The group considered whether day cases should be counted as admissions.

**7.2** Finance stated that day cases must be counted as admissions, because the tariff is paid
on an admission basis and a day case that is not counted is a day case that is not paid for.

**7.3** Clinical coding stated that day cases must not be counted as admissions, because the
national coding standard defines an admission as requiring an overnight stay, and that a local
variation would make the hospital's returns incomparable with every other hospital's.

**7.4** Both positions were agreed to be correct.

**7.5** It was agreed that each department would continue to use its own definition for its own
purposes, and that the matter would be reviewed when a single reporting system was introduced.
Mr Vergara noted that this effectively meant the hospital had two admission figures. The chair
noted that it had always had two admission figures and that the meeting had simply written that
down.

**7.6** **ACTION:** Information manager to prepare a note setting out both definitions, for
circulation to all departments.

---

*Archivist's note, added 2024: no note was circulated. The single reporting system was introduced
in 2019 and uses the finance definition without stating that it does so. The clinical coding
definition remains in use for national returns. Item 7 has not been reviewed.*

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What was the question in front of the group?
2. **What was finance's argument, and what was clinical coding's?**
3. What did the group conclude about the two positions?
4. What was agreed, and what did Mr Vergara point out?
5. **What did the chair say, and what is the force of it?**
6. What action was agreed?
7. What does the archivist's note say happened?
8. **Which definition is now in use, and does the system say so?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> on an admission basis · a local variation · incomparable · effectively meant ·
> without stating that it does so

### 5E · The counter-text


![A hospital report page with a headline waiting-time figure and the four different departmental figures for the same month, with the sources of each.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The report page, and the four departmental figures — One sentence, no definition, and four numbers that are all correct.*


**Read the report page and the four departmental figures, and answer.**

1. What does the headline sentence say, and what does it not say?
2. **What are the four figures, and what is the range between them?**
3. Where, if anywhere, is a definition given?
4. Which of the four departments' figures is used in the regional return?
5. One sticky note has been attached to the page. What does it say?

### 5F · Two texts

The 2011 minutes record **an agreed action** to circulate both definitions.
The report has **no definition anywhere on it**.

**Put those two facts together. Whose fault is the report?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Pointing precisely

### 6A · Sort — narrowing, generalising, or pointing back?

Three columns.

> as a whole · such · the latter · the former · given · any · respective · specific ·
> generic · certain · particular

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **In the ______** , the two figures agree to within fifty.
2. **A kind ______** working definition has been in use since 2011.
3. **The sort ______** patient this excludes is the one who comes in and goes home.
4. **The one in ______** is the regional return.
5. What does *admission* **stand ______** in this system?
6. The paragraph **refers ______** a definition that is not in the document.

### 6C · Three that look like precision

**certain · given · particular**

> *Certain* means *some, and I am not telling you which*. *Given* means *specified, in a formula*.
> *Particular* means *this one, as opposed to others*.

**Which fits each sentence, and what changes?**

1. ______ patients are excluded from this figure.
2. For a ______ month, the figure is produced on the fifth working day.
3. This ______ figure is the one in the regional return.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **Which patients, exactly?**

Three words that stop a meeting. **Write the two sentences before it and the sentence after it,**
in a meeting where it is welcome and in one where it is not.

Eight items. Choose the article or determiner, and name the meaning.

2. ______ patient is rarely consulted. *(the category, formal)*
3. ______ departments produced their own figures. *(each its own)*
4. Of the two, ______ is lower. *(the second)*
5. For a ______ month, the return is due on the fifth. *(in a formula)*
6. ______ patients waited 41 days. *(this group)*
7. ______ patients wait too long. *(everybody)*
8. ______ patients are excluded. *(the kind just described)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Two words, four sounds


![Five phrases showing the as a schwa, the as /i:/, stressed THE, a as a schwa and stressed A.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Two words, four sounds — The and a each have a weak form, a strong form, and a stressed form that means something else.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 8.3

> the patients → /ðə/ — before a consonant
> the only ones → /ðiː/ — before a vowel sound
> **THE** patients → /ðiː/ — stressed, meaning *the real ones*
> a patient → /ə/ · **A** patient, not two → /eɪ/

### 7B · Say

Say all four. Then say the sentence three times, faster:

> *The patients in the appendix are the only ones the definition covers.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *It's a definition.* *(one of several)*
> *It's THE definition.* *(and there is no other)*

**In the 2011 minutes, which of the two is true?**

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four generic statements about any group you like, one of each kind.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A category, explained · 160 words


![A diagram of one month of hospital admissions drawn in layers, from overnight stays through day cases to emergency attendances, with which definition includes each.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One month of admissions, in section — Four layers, and the dispute lives entirely in the second one.*


### 8A · Read the model

> An admission, in this report, means any patient given a bed number, whether or not they stayed
> overnight. `[the definition, in the first sentence, and it names the test: a bed number]`
>
> That includes day cases, which most people would not call an admission, and about four hundred
> of them a month fall into this figure. It excludes patients seen and discharged from the
> emergency department without a bed number, of whom there are about nine hundred.
> `[one surprising inclusion and one surprising exclusion, both with numbers]`
>
> Clinical coding uses the national standard, which requires an overnight stay. Their figure for
> the same month is 1,340 lower. Neither definition is wrong.
> `[the other definition, the size of the gap, and a refusal to adjudicate]`
>
> If somebody quotes an admission figure at you, the only useful question is whether a day case
> is in it. `[what the reader should do, in one sentence]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The definition names a physical test — a bed number. **Why is that better than a principle?**
2. Both the inclusion and the exclusion have numbers. **What would be lost without them?**
3. The last sentence is an instruction. **Who is it for?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Admissions data is collected in accordance with agreed definitions and is subject to ongoing
> review. Figures may vary between departments due to differing methodologies. Users should
> exercise caution when making comparisons.

Correct English, and it will not help anybody. **Name three things it does not say**,
and say what *differing methodologies* is standing in for.

### 8C · Plan

> the definition, with a physical test → one surprising inclusion, with a number →
> one surprising exclusion, with a number → the other definition and the size of the gap →
> the one question a reader should ask

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Can a reader now sort a case into the category or out of it?
> ☐ Is there an inclusion and an exclusion, both with numbers?
> ☐ Does the piece avoid saying one definition is right?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the report page from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# One figure, four readings

A report says *the patients waited an average of 41 days*. Four departments each read it as their
own population. Their figures range from 31 to 58.

- **Publish a definition.** One sentence at the top of the report. It would make the figure
  comparable and it would make this year's figure look worse than last year's, which was
  produced the other way.
- **Publish all four.** Honest, unusable, and the regional office will pick one.
- **Leave it.** Everybody carries on reading their own number, as they have since 2011, and the
  report continues to be quoted in four meetings a month.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of four departmental populations and the average waiting time each produces for the same month.](figures/fig_b12_u08_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*The same month, four definitions — Population counted, and average wait produced, by each of the four departments.*


1. **The report page and the four figures** from Part 5E — and the sticky note.
2. **The chart** — the four populations, their overlaps, and the four hundred people in dispute.
3. **The voice.** The information manager, thirty seconds: *"I was asked to write that note in
   2011. I did write it. I could not find anybody who would own it, because owning it would mean
   choosing, and the two people who would have had to agree had both correctly been told that
   their definition was the right one."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *By definition ______ . In practice ______ . So the figure means ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *category · definition · criterion · boundary · overlap · ambiguity ·
classification · in principle · for the purposes of · fall under*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the hospital should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Publish the definition and publish last year's figure recalculated on the same basis, in the
> same table. The reason this has survived thirteen years is that fixing it makes somebody's
> number worse, and the only way to remove that cost is to restate the comparison at the same
> time. One sentence and one recalculated column turn an embarrassment into a correction, and a
> correction is a thing an organisation can survive.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The definition went in, with the recalculated prior year beside it. The
> figure for this year rose from 41 to 47 days and last year's rose from 39 to 46, so the
> direction of travel was unchanged and the headline was *waiting times up by one day*.
>
> Three departments adopted the published definition within a quarter. Clinical coding did not,
> and could not, because the national standard binds them.
>
> The report is still quoted in four meetings a month. In two of them somebody now asks which
> definition, which the information manager describes as the entire return on thirteen years.

**One sentence and one column. Three of four departments. And somebody now asks the question in
half the meetings.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what should be done about clinical coding.

# How many is most?

**By the end of this unit you can present a set of figures and hedge them correctly.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A number that is accurate, defensible and will be read wrongly |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | advanced quantifiers with *of* · hedged quantity · 23 words for statistics · 22 words for approximation · stress used for contrast |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | A set of figures, hedged to exactly the strength the data supports |

*Ninety-four per cent — One of them has to write the press line. One of them has read the letter.*

**Before you listen.** The hospital's waiting-list figure is 94 per cent within target. It is
accurate. **Guess: what does it hide?**

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What they want to publish | Why | What they are afraid of |
|---|---|---|---|
| the communications lead | | | |
| Dr Herrera | | | |
| the information manager | | | |

**Listen again. Choose the best answer.**

1. The six per cent is about **40 / 400 / 4,000** people.
2. Their median wait is **nine months / six weeks / two years**.
3. The 94 per cent figure is **rounded up / wrong / right**.

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

2. *The vast majority are seen within six weeks.*
3. *Roughly nine out of ten are seen within six weeks.*

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Saying how many, and how sure

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, could you say *Most them*? In sentence 2, could you say *The vast majority of*?
2. Sentence 3 uses *roughly*. **What is it hedging — the count, or the proportion?**
3. Sentence 4 counts the exception instead of the rule. **What does that change for the reader?**
4. **Which of the four would a patients' group object to, and on what grounds?**
5. Try *"The majority of patient is seen within six weeks."* **What is wrong?**

### 3B · See


![A ladder of quantifier phrases from the most reassuring at the top to the one that counts the exception at the bottom.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Five ways of saying ninety-four per cent — All five are accurate. They do not leave the reader in the same place.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **most** | *most patients* · *most **of** the patients* | *of* only before *the*, a possessive or a pronoun |
| **the majority** | *the majority **of** patients* | *of* is compulsory |
| **the bulk of** | *the bulk **of** the work* | uncountable or collective |
| **a handful of** | *a handful **of** cases* | countable, small, and slightly dismissive |
| **next to none** | *next to none **of** them* | a hedge on zero |
| **all but** | *all but four hundred* | counts the exception |

> **Verb agreement.** *The majority* and *the bulk* take the verb of what follows:
> *the majority of patients **are*** · *the majority of the work **is***.

> **The hedges, from tightest to loosest:**
> *marginally · slightly · somewhat · broadly · roughly · approximately · in the region of ·
> or so · give or take*

> **Degree words are not hedges.** *substantially*, *considerably*, *notably* and *largely*
> make a claim **bigger**, not vaguer, and a reader will hold you to them.

> **Why it matters.** A hedge that is looser than your data is dishonest in one direction.
> A hedge that is tighter is dishonest in the other, and is the commoner mistake.

### 3D · Use — controlled

> (1) __________ of patients are seen within six weeks. More precisely,
> (2) __________ majority — (3) __________ ninety-four per cent — are. The remaining six per
> cent is (4) __________ four hundred people, and their median wait is nine months.
> (5) __________ but a handful of those four hundred are on three specific pathways. The figure
> has risen (6) __________ since 2019, by about two points, which is not
> (7) __________ a change as the chart makes it look. Spending per head
> (8) __________ out at roughly eleven per cent below the regional average.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one with the quantifier in brackets, and say what changes.

1. 94 per cent are seen in six weeks. *(the vast majority)*
2. 94 per cent are seen in six weeks. *(all but about four hundred)*
3. Six per cent are not. *(a significant minority)*
4. Two of the 400 were seen in under a year. *(next to none)*
5. The rise is 2.1 points. *(marginally)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Take one figure. Present it **four times**: to reassure, to alarm, to inform,
and to mislead without lying. **Your partner names which is which**, and then says which of the
four you would sign.

### 3G · Error autopsy


![Two panels comparing a claim of substantial improvement with the actual change, showing the size of the claim against the size of the evidence.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*A hedge that is tighter than the data — A hedge that is tighter than the data*


**Read both versions. Then say which of the two the data actually supports.**

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ of the patients are seen in six weeks.
> 2. The ______ of patients ______ seen in six weeks. *(majority + verb)*
> 3. The bulk of the work ______ done at night. *(verb)*
> 4. ______ but four hundred are seen in time.
> 5. ______ to none of them were seen in under a year.
> 6. It has risen ______ — by about two points. *(tightest hedge)*
> 7. Spending per head works ______ at eleven per cent below.
> 8. A ______ of cases account for most of the delay.

**0–4 →** Workbook pp. 52–55. **5–6 →** Part 6. **7–8 →** page 195.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — the same number, five ways

Your teacher gives a percentage. **Each learner must present it with a different quantifier**,
and the group ranks the five by how reassuring they sound.
**Four percentages. Four rounds.**

### 4B · The functional core — presenting figures honestly


![Two panels compared: a pie of percentages and a column of raw counts for the same population.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The percentages, and the counts — Learner A has the per cents. Learner B has the people. One group is 1 per cent and 400.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the headline** | *The vast majority…* · *Roughly nine in ten…* |
| **the exception** | *All but about four hundred…* · *A significant minority…* |
| **the denominator** | *Out of 6,700…* · *That is per head of population.* |
| **the honest qualification** | *The median is a better number here.* · *True and misleading.* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the percentages. Learner B has the raw counts.
Neither sees the other. **Find the group that is 1 per cent and 400 people.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Present **a set of figures**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

> the headline figure, hedged correctly · the denominator · the exception, counted in people ·
> the measure you would use instead, and why

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person listens for **one thing**: was any hedge tighter than the data allowed?

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I use *of* correctly every time?
> ☐ Is every percentage attached to a denominator?
> ☐ Did I count the exception in people as well as per cent?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

A letter and a reply. The title is **"Re: your figure of 94 per cent."**
**What do you expect the patients' group to be asking for?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a quarter

---

**From: Valdivia Patients' Forum**
**To: the Chief Executive**
**Re: your figure of 94 per cent**

We are not writing to say the figure is wrong. We have checked it and it is right.

We are writing because of what happens to the six per cent. There are about four hundred of them.
Their median wait is nine months. Fifty-one of them have waited more than two years. They are not
distributed across the service: they are concentrated on three pathways, and one of those three
is the one with no consultant.

Every time the 94 per cent figure is published, each of those four hundred people reads a
sentence saying that the service is meeting its target, and each of them is one of the people it
is not meeting it for. That is not a complaint about arithmetic. It is a complaint about what a
number is for.

We are asking for one thing: that the figure be published with the median wait of the group who
are outside it, in the same sentence. Not a footnote. The same sentence.

---

**From: the Chief Executive**
**Re: your letter**

You are right and we will do it.

I want to say one thing about why we have not. The 94 per cent figure is the one the regional
office asks for, in that form, and it is the form in which we are compared with eleven other
hospitals. If we publish it with the median of the excluded group beside it, we will be the only
hospital doing so, and we will look worse than hospitals that are performing worse than we are.

I have decided that I would rather look worse than be the hospital whose four hundred longest
waits are not written down anywhere a patient can find them. I am aware this is easy to say in a
letter and that I will be asked about it in March.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What does the Forum say first, and why does it matter?
2. **How many people are in the six per cent, and what is their median wait?**
3. How are they distributed, and what is notable about one of the three pathways?
4. What is the Forum's complaint actually about?
5. **What exactly do they ask for, and what do they explicitly refuse?**
6. What is the Chief Executive's explanation for not having done it?
7. **What is the comparison problem, and why is it real?**
8. What does the last sentence of the reply acknowledge?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> distributed across · concentrated on · what a number is for · in that form ·
> easy to say in a letter

### 5E · The counter-text


![A hospital waiting-list figures pack showing the headline percentage, the denominator, median waits, the long-wait tail and a regional comparison table.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The figures pack — Eleven rows, one of them struck through and reinstated, and the median on page three.*


**Read the figures pack and answer.**

1. What is the headline figure, and what is its denominator?
2. **What is the median wait of the whole list, and of the six per cent?**
3. How many people have waited more than two years, and on which pathways?
4. What does the regional comparison table show about this hospital?
5. One row has been struck through and reinstated. Which, and what does the note say?

### 5F · Two texts

The reply says publishing both numbers will make the hospital **look worse than hospitals
performing worse**.
The comparison table shows this hospital **fourth of twelve**.

**Put those two facts together. Is the Chief Executive right?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Hedging to exactly the right strength

### 6A · Sort — tighter than, looser than, or bigger than?

Three columns.

> substantially · comparatively · marginally · slightly · notably · broadly ·
> approximately · considerably · largely · roughly · somewhat

**Four of them belong in the third column and are regularly used as if they belonged in the
second.** Which?

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **In the ______ of** eleven per cent below the regional average.
2. **The best ______ of** a year, for fifty-one of them.
3. **Next to ______** of the four hundred were seen inside twelve months.
4. **The ______ of** the delay sits on three pathways.
5. The totals **come ______** 6,700, **give or ______** forty.
6. The rate **works ______ at** about one in seventeen.

### 6C · Three that are not hedges

**substantially · considerably · largely**

> All three make a claim **bigger**. A reader will ask you to support them, and a hedge is
> supposed to do the opposite.

**Rewrite each sentence with a real hedge, and say what you have given up.**

1. Waits have increased substantially.
2. The figure is considerably better than last year.
3. The delay is largely on three pathways.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **About a third, give or take.**

**Say it twice:** once where you have counted and once where you have not.
**Can a listener tell?** What would make it honest in the second case?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Choose the quantifier or hedge, and justify the strength.

1. A rise of 2.1 points. *(tightest honest hedge)*
2. Three pathways out of eleven. *(where the delay sits)*
3. 6,700 ± 40. *(spoken)*
4. 6 per cent. *(as a minority worth noticing)*
5. 2 out of 400. *(a hedge on zero)*
6. 94 per cent. *(counting the exception)*
7. 11 per cent below. *(approximate, spoken)*
8. 94 per cent. *(reassuring)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Stress as the argument


![Five versions of one statistic sentence with different syllables stressed, each answering a different unspoken objection.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Stress as the argument — One sentence, four stresses, four different things being denied.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 9.3

> **NINE**ty-four per cent are seen in time. — and you said ninety
> Ninety-four per cent **ARE** seen in time. — and you implied they were not
> Ninety-four per cent of **PATIENTS** — and not of referrals
> Ninety-four per cent of patients **ARE SEEN** — not treated

### 7B · Say

Say all four. Then say the sentence three times, faster, moving the stress each time.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *Most of them are seen in six weeks.* *(flat)*
> *MOST of them are seen in six weeks.* *(and some are not, and that is the point)*

**Which one would the patients' group write, and which would the press office?**

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four figures about this room, each with a different hedge.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A set of figures · 160 words


![A waiting list drawn in four layers from those seen within six weeks down to those waiting over two years, with counts on each.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*The waiting list, in section — Four layers. The headline describes the top one and the letter is about the bottom.*


### 8A · Read the model

> Of 6,700 patients referred last year, 94 per cent were seen within six weeks. The denominator
> is referrals accepted, not referrals received; about 310 were returned and are not in this
> figure. `[the headline, the denominator, and the thing the denominator excludes]`
>
> The remaining six per cent is 402 people. Their median wait is nine months and fifty-one of
> them have waited more than two years. `[the exception, in people, with a median rather than an
> average]`
>
> Next to none of those 402 are spread across the service. The bulk of them — about 340 — are on
> three pathways, one of which has had no consultant since January. `[where it sits, with the
> cause named]`
>
> The 94 per cent is accurate. The median of the 402 is the number I would want if I were on one
> of those three pathways. `[a sentence that does not retract the figure and does not defend it
> either]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The denominator is named in the first paragraph, with the 310 it excludes.
   **Why not in a footnote?**
2. The exception is given in people and the headline in per cent. **Why that way round?**
3. The last sentence does not choose. **Is that evasion?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> We are pleased to report that the vast majority of patients continue to be seen well within
> target. Waiting times have improved considerably over the period and we remain committed to
> further reductions. A small number of patients experience longer waits.

Correct English, and three of its four claims are unsupportable as written.
**Find them**, and say what *a small number* is doing.

### 8C · Plan

> the headline with its denominator → what the denominator excludes → the exception, in people,
> with a median → where it is concentrated, and why → one sentence that neither retracts nor
> defends

### 8D · Write — 160 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every percentage attached to a denominator?
> ☐ Is the exception counted in people?
> ☐ Is every hedge exactly as strong as the data?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the figures pack from Part 5E beside it.

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# CULTURE FILE · Naming, Address and Status


![Four letter openings to the same chief executive, compared by form of address, hedge count and speed of reply.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four letters, four openings — The same request. The form of address and the hedging do most of the work.*


### 9A · Four letters, four openings 🔊 Track 9.4

The same request, written by four people to the same Chief Executive. **Listen to the openings
read aloud.**

1. Which one uses a first name, and what does the writer risk?
2. **Which one is so deferential that the request disappears?**
3. Which one gets the quickest reply, and is that the same as the best one?

### 9B · What a form of address claims

> *Dear Dr Soto* · *Dear María* · *Dear Chief Executive* · *To whom it may concern*

**Each of the four makes a claim about the relationship.** Say what each claims.
Then say which one a patients' group should use, and why it is not obvious.

### 9C · Status in three languages

In some languages the title does the work. In some the verb does. In English a great deal is
carried by **how much hedging you use**, and a reader hears over-hedging as either deference or
evasion.

**Which of these four is most likely to be read as evasion by an English reader?**

1. *I wonder whether it might perhaps be possible to consider publishing the median.*
2. *Please publish the median.*
3. *We are asking for one thing: publish the median in the same sentence.*
4. *It would be appreciated if the median could be published.*

### 9D · Your turn

In pairs. **Write the same two-sentence request four times:** to somebody senior you have never
met, to somebody senior you know well, to a peer, and to an institution with no name.
**Then swap, and your partner says which is which.**

### 9E · Transfer

**Find one email you have sent this week.** Count the hedges in the first sentence, and say what
they were doing.

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Publish, annotate, or hold

The 94 per cent figure is due for publication. It is accurate. Four hundred and two people are
outside it with a median wait of nine months.

- **Publish as it stands.** It is the regional format, it is comparable with eleven other
  hospitals, and it is true.
- **Publish with the median beside it.** What the Forum asked for. This hospital becomes the only
  one doing it and drops from fourth of twelve to ninth on the published comparison.
- **Hold it until the next quarter.** The consultant post on the worst pathway is being filled in
  March. One quarter's delay and the number improves on its own.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart of waiting times across the whole list showing a large group under six weeks and a long thin tail past a year.](figures/fig_b12_u09_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*The whole distribution, not the headline — Six thousand seven hundred patients, by how long they waited.*


1. **The figures pack** from Part 5E — and the row that was struck through and reinstated.
2. **The chart** — the whole distribution, not the headline.
3. **The voice.** One of the 402, thirty seconds: *"I am not asking anybody to say the figure is
   wrong. I have read it and it is right. I would like it to be possible for somebody to find
   out, without writing a letter, that there are four hundred of us, because at the moment the
   only way I know is that I wrote a letter."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *The vast majority ______ . A significant minority ______ . It depends on ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *statistic · proportion · median · average · outlier · denominator ·
cohort · distribution · true and misleading · the bulk of · all but*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think the hospital should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> Publish both, in the same sentence, and write to the regional office the same day proposing
> that the second figure become standard. Dropping to ninth is a real cost and it is a cost paid
> by the hospital's reputation, which is the thing that has least claim to be protected here.
> The letter to the region is the part that matters: one hospital publishing an extra number
> looks like an admission, and twelve publishing it is a format.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** Both figures were published in the same sentence. The hospital dropped to
> ninth of twelve on the comparison table and was asked about it twice in March, once by a
> journalist and once by the regional office.
>
> The letter proposing the second figure as standard was acknowledged and not answered for
> fourteen months. Two other hospitals adopted it voluntarily in that period, both of them after
> being contacted by their own patients' forums, who had read this one's letter.
>
> The regional format changed in the following year. It now requires the median of the group
> outside the target. Nine of the twelve hospitals got worse on the published table; the ranking
> barely moved.

**They looked worse for fourteen months. Then the format changed and the ranking barely moved,
because everybody had the same four hundred.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the fourteen months cost.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Wishing about now, and about then

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2.

1. In sentence 1, does she know? In sentence 2?
2. **Why is the verb in the past in sentence 1 when the wish is about now?**
3. Sentence 3 uses *if only*. **Is it stronger or weaker than *I wish*?**
4. In sentence 4, *I'd rather somebody said it* — why a past tense after *would rather*?
5. Try *"I wish I know."* **Why does English refuse this?**

### 3B · See


![A ladder of wish forms from wish plus past simple about the present up through wish plus past perfect about the past and would rather.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Wishing, about now and about then — The past tense marks distance from reality, not past time. That is the whole system.*


### 3C · Know

| | Shape | About |
|---|---|---|
| **wish + past simple** | *I wish I **knew**.* | now — and I do not |
| **wish + past perfect** | *I wish I**'d known**.* | the past — and I did not |
| **wish + *would*** | *I wish he**'d stop** saying that.* | somebody else's behaviour, now — a complaint |
| **wish + could** | *I wish I **could** tell you.* | ability, now |
| **if only** | same shapes, more force | ***If only** I'd known.* |
| **would rather + past** | *I'd rather you **didn't**.* | what you want somebody else to do |
| **suppose / supposing** | + past, for a hypothesis | ***Suppose** we **asked** him.* |

> **The past tense is doing the same job as in the second conditional:** it marks distance from
> reality, not past time.

> ***wish + would* cannot be used about yourself.**
> ✗ *I wish I would stop.* ✓ *I wish I could stop.* ✓ *I wish he would stop.*
> It is a complaint about somebody else's choices, and you cannot complain about your own.

> **Watch out.** ✗ *I wish I know.* ✗ *I wish I would know.* ✓ *I wish I knew.*

### 3D · Use — controlled

> I wish I (1) __________ (know) which one to choose. I wish somebody
> (2) __________ (tell) me in the first year, when it would have made a difference. If only the
> rota (3) __________ (not / change) every February — I wish they
> (4) __________ (stop) doing that, honestly. I'd rather somebody just
> (5) __________ (say) it to me. Suppose we (6) __________ (ask) the three people who left:
> I wish we (7) __________ (can) do that, and I wish I (8) __________ (think) of it two
> years ago.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one as a *wish* or an *if only*.

1. I don't know what I want. *(now)*
2. Nobody told me. *(the past)*
3. He keeps saying that. *(a complaint)*
4. I can't tell you. *(ability, now)*
5. I didn't ask her. *(the past, with force)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** Name three things you wish you knew now and three you wish you had known then.
**Your partner's job** is to say which of the six could actually have been taught.

![Two panels comparing I wish I would stop with I wish I could stop, showing who the verb is a complaint about.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p03_v11.png){width=6.6in}

*The wish English will not allow — The wish English will not allow*


**Read both versions. Then say why English will not allow the first one.**

> 1. I wish I ______ (know). *(now)*
> 2. I wish somebody ______ (tell) me. *(the past)*
> 3. I wish he ______ (stop) saying that. *(a complaint)*
> 4. I wish I ______ (can) help. *(ability, now)*
> 5. If only I ______ (ask) her. *(the past)*

Your teacher names a domain: *your first week · your training · your last job*.

| | |
|---|---|

Describe **a gap in what you were taught**. **Three minutes.** You must include:

One person listens for **one thing**: did the speaker blame a person or name a gap?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

An extract from a report on first-year doctors. The title is
**"The curriculum is not the problem."**
**What do you expect the report to say the problem is?** Two sentences.

### 5B · Read — two minutes and a half

---

**THE CURRICULUM IS NOT THE PROBLEM**
*Extract from a review of first-year clinical experience*

**4.1** We asked 211 doctors in their first year what they wished they had been taught. We
expected clinical answers. We received eleven.

**4.2** The remaining answers divided into three groups.

**4.3** The first group — about half of all answers — concerned **who to ask**. Not what to do:
who to ask. Several respondents described spending between twenty and fifty minutes locating a
person who could answer a question that took ninety seconds to answer. One wrote: *I wish
somebody had drawn me a map of who knows what.*

**4.4** The second group concerned **how to say that you do not know**. Respondents described
learning this by one of two routes: either a senior colleague said *I don't know* in front of
them in the first fortnight, in which case they could do it from then on, or nobody did, in which
case most of them reported still finding it difficult several years later. It appears to be
almost entirely a matter of modelling, and the modelling either happens in the first two weeks or
does not happen.

**4.5** The third group concerned **what is normal**. Respondents reported being unable to tell,
for about a year, whether a thing that felt wrong was wrong or was simply how hospitals are.
Several said they had stopped reporting things in their second year, not because they had stopped
noticing but because they had stopped being able to tell.

**4.6** None of these three is in any curriculum. Two of the three cannot obviously be put in
one. The first can, costs almost nothing, and is the one nobody does.

**4.7** We note, without comment, that 190 of the 211 responses described something the
institution could have provided and did not, and that the institution has asked us three times
whether the clinical content is adequate.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many doctors were asked, and how many gave clinical answers?
2. **What was the first group of answers about, and what example is given?**
3. What are the two routes by which the second thing is learned?
4. **What does paragraph 4.4 conclude about when this can be learned?**
5. What was the third group about, and what did several respondents say had happened by their
   second year?
6. Which of the three could be put in a curriculum?
7. **What is the force of paragraph 4.7?**
8. Why does the report say "without comment"?

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Guess from the text. Then check.

> locating a person · a matter of modelling · what is normal · stopped being able to tell ·
> without comment

### 5E · The counter-text


![A first-year curriculum contents page listing clinical domains and hours, alongside a summary of what 211 first-year doctors said they wished they had been taught.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The curriculum contents page, and the survey answers — Eight hundred and twenty hours, fourteen domains, and three findings that are in none of them.*


**Read the curriculum contents page and the survey answers beside it, and answer.**

1. How many hours does the curriculum allocate, and to how many domains?
2. **Which three of the survey's answers appear nowhere in the curriculum?**
3. What does the curriculum call the nearest thing to "who to ask", and how many hours does it
   get?
4. What has been added to the curriculum in the last five years, and what has been removed?
5. One line of the contents page has been annotated by hand. What does it say?

### 5F · Two texts

The report says the first gap **costs almost nothing to fix**.
The curriculum gives the nearest domain **two hours, in week nine**.

**Put those two facts together. What would fixing it actually require?** Three sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Supposing, and preferring

### 6A · Sort — a wish, a preference, or a hypothesis?

Three columns.

> supposedly · frankly · regrettably · wish · ideally · rather · preferably · if only ·
> suppose · hypothetically · admittedly

**Two of them are not about the future at all and are regularly used as if they were.** Which?

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **If ______** somebody had said it in the first week.
2. **I'd ______** know now than find out in March.
3. **Suppose ______** asked the three people who left.
4. **It would be better ______** the rota changed in June.
5. **In an ideal ______** , somebody would draw the map.
6. He has gone **______ over** his first year four times trying to date it.

### 6C · Three that are not hedges

**supposedly · admittedly · frankly**

> *Supposedly* reports somebody else's claim and doubts it. *Admittedly* concedes a point
> against yourself. *Frankly* announces that what follows is impolite.

**Which fits each sentence, and what would the others do?**

1. ______ , the clinical content is adequate.
2. ______ , I did not ask anybody either.
3. ______ , the review is about something else.

### 6D · The fixed expression

> **f** **If only somebody had told me.**

**Write the sentence that follows it,** once from somebody who would have listened, and once
from somebody who would not.

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Give the *wish*, *if only*, *would rather* or *suppose* form.

1. I don't know. *(now)*
2. Nobody told me. *(the past)*
3. What if we asked them? *(hypothesis)*
4. I can't help. *(ability, now)*
5. I didn't ask. *(with force)*
6. She keeps changing it. *(a complaint)*
7. I was too sure in my first year. *(the past, about yourself)*
8. Please don't say anything. *(preference, politely)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The tune of a wish


![Three pitch contours of wish sentences, one closed and falling, one open and rising, and one with two falls.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The tune of a wish — A wish that falls is finished. A wish that rises has a but coming.*


### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 10.3

> *I wish I knew.* ↘ — closed, and there is nothing after it
> *I wish I knew…* ↗ — open, and a *but* is coming
> *If only somebody had told me.* ↘↘ — two falls, and the second is lower

### 7B · Say

Say all three. Then say the sentence twice, once closed and once open:

> *I wish I'd asked her.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *I WISH I'd known.* *(and somebody has just implied you did)*
> *I wish I'd KNOWN.* *(and the knowing is the thing that was missing)*

**Which of the two is a defence?**

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **three wishes about now and three about then.**
Three times, faster, with the fall at the end of each.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### What nobody taught me · 180 words


![A first clinical year drawn in four layers from taught clinical content down to the things learned in corridors, with hours and shares marked.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p08_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*A first year, in section — Four layers. The curriculum covers the top one and the survey is about the other three.*


### 8A · Read the model

> I wish somebody had told me who to ask. Not what to do — I was taught what to do, at length,
> and most of it was right. `[the gap, and a sentence that protects the curriculum so the reader
> knows this is not a complaint about teaching]`
>
> In my first four months I spent, at a conservative estimate, eleven hours looking for people. I
> once took forty minutes to find somebody who answered my question in under two.
> `[the cost, in hours, with one specific instance]`
>
> I wish I knew whether this is normal. I have asked four people and got four answers, and three
> of them began by saying that it was different in their day.
> `[a wish about now, which is unresolved, and a detail that shows why]`
>
> What I do not think could have been taught is how to say I don't know. I learned it in my
> second week, from somebody who said it in front of me in a corridor, and I do not think a
> lecture would have done it. `[the honest limit, with the mechanism named]`
>
> I would rather have been given a map than another lecture. That is the whole of what I have to
> say.

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The second sentence defends the curriculum. **What does that buy the writer?**
2. The cost is given in hours. **Why not in frustration?**
3. The fourth paragraph says something could not have been taught. **Does that weaken the piece?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Looking back on my first year, it was a steep learning curve and I learned a great deal.
> There were challenges but they made me the clinician I am today. I would say to anyone
> starting out: ask for help, be kind to yourself, and remember that everyone feels like this.

Correct English, and nothing in it could be acted on. **Name three things that are missing**,
and say what *a steep learning curve* is standing in for.

### 8C · Plan

> the gap, named precisely → one sentence that protects what was taught → the cost, with a
> number → a wish about now that is unresolved → one thing that could not have been taught →
> what you would rather have had

### 8D · Write — 180 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is the gap specific enough for somebody to fix it?
> ☐ Is there a cost with a number in it?
> ☐ Is there one thing the writer admits could not have been taught?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

On the wall, with the curriculum contents page from Part 5E beside it.

> naming your own position · leaving the decision where it belongs

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Needed, or good

Matías has to choose a speciality. There are nine unfilled posts in the region in one and two in
the other. He is measurably better at the one with two.

- **Where he is needed.** Nine posts, a job anywhere, a shortage that is somebody's emergency,
  and work he is competent at and does not love.
- **Where he is good.** Two posts, a competitive application, and the thing three separate
  supervisors have independently said he has a feel for.
- **Neither yet.** A year in a general post, which delays the decision, costs a year of training,
  and is what about a fifth of his year group does.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing unfilled posts, applicants per post and four-year attrition in two specialities over a decade.](figures/fig_b12_u10_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Posts, applicants and attrition, over ten years — Two specialities, and the shortage is not a recruitment problem.*


1. **The curriculum and the survey** from Part 5E — and the hand annotation.
2. **The chart** — posts, applicants and attrition in each speciality over ten years.
3. **The voice.** Dr Herrera, thirty seconds: *"I chose the one I was needed in, in 2007, because
   of a train. I have been good at it for eighteen years and I would make the same decision now
   and I would like somebody to notice that those are two different sentences."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In fours. Everybody speaks twice. Use the frame.

> *I wish ______ . If only ______ . So I think ______ .*

**Words you can use:** *gap · insight · competence · confidence · mentorship · out of my depth ·
on the job · with hindsight · the hard way · a steep learning curve*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**. Choose, say why, and name what it costs.

> I think Matías should ______________________ ,
> because ______________________ .
> What this costs is ______________________ .

### 11D · Model decision

> He should take the one he is good at and should say out loud, now, that he is doing it, because
> the shortage speciality's nine vacancies are a real emergency and the only honest thing is to
> decline them in public rather than quietly. Shortages are solved by making the work better, not
> by recruiting people who will be adequate at it and leave in four years, and the attrition
> figures say exactly that. He owes the region a clear no, which is more than most people give it.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** He took the one he is good at. He wrote a four-line note to the regional
> training lead explaining why he had not applied to the shortage speciality, which he was told
> was unnecessary and which he sent anyway.
>
> The training lead quoted it, anonymously, in a paper to the regional board about why the
> shortage is not a recruitment problem. Two other students sent similar notes the following
> year.
>
> He is in his third year and has twice been close to moving. He says the thing that keeps him is
> not that he is good at it but that in his first fortnight somebody said *I don't know* in front
> of him in a corridor, and that he has been able to say it ever since, and that he does not
> think he would have been able to in the other department.

**He chose the thing he was good at. The reason he has stayed is something that happened in a
corridor in his second week.**

> **Was it the right decision?** Say why — and say what the four-line note was actually for.

### 12E · Glossary — forty-five items, as a map

> **THE GAP** · gap · ignorance · blind spot · out of my depth · nobody told me
> **WHAT YOU GET** · curriculum · apprenticeship · training · mentorship · feedback · lesson
> **WHAT YOU BUILD** · insight · competence · confidence · reflection · pick up · work out · catch up · fall behind
> **HOW IT FEELS** · a steep learning curve · on the job · the hard way · with hindsight
> **WHAT PEOPLE SAY** · I wish somebody had said · you learn that in week one or never · if only somebody had told me
> **WISHING** · wish · if only · I wish I knew · looking back · think back · go over
> **PREFERRING AND SUPPOSING** · rather · I'd rather · suppose · suppose we · it would be better if · in an ideal world

# Answer Key
