## B1.2 · Unit 1 · What the Canteen Buys

**0B** · Guess it
Costing the hospital almost nothing: beans and rice, and tinned fruit — both bought in bulk, stored dry, and priced close to what they cost. Costing it a great deal: the fish of the day and the subsidised staff meal. **No** — the board does not tell you. The staff meal is priced at 45 per cent of a number that is itself below cost, and the only sentence on either menu that mentions what anything costs the hospital is the note at the foot of the staff copy.

**1B** · Sort — the food, the money, or the habit?
> **the habit** — generation · household
> **the money** — budget · portion
> **the food** — canteen · processed · staple · seasonal · recipe
**The one that moves:** *seasonal*. With money it is a description of the food — what is good this month. With less of it, it becomes a budget word: *seasonal* starts meaning *whatever is cheap*, and the menu stops naming the fruit.

**2A** · The canteen budget 🔊 Track 1.1
**1** b) 400 · **2** c) subsidised · **3** b) 11 · **4** a) dignity
**Table** — Rosa wants the cheaper menu; her reason is that the budget has fallen 19 per cent in real terms; what she is not saying is that the saving falls almost entirely on the staff meal. Dr Herrera wants the fish kept; his reason is nutrition; what he is not saying is that he eats at lunch and the night shift does not. Ignacio wants the night service kept as it is; his reason is that people on nights have nowhere else; what he is not saying is that he has eaten at three in the morning for nineteen years and is describing his own life.

**Noticing** — Sentences 1 and 2 are the stopped habits: *used to* for a past state and *would* for a repeated past action. Sentence 3 (*I'm used to*) describes a present state of familiarity; sentence 4 (*get used to*) describes the process of becoming familiar. Neither of the last two is about the past at all.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 used to cook · 2 would come · 3 would leave · 4 used to be · 5 was not used to · 6 got used to

**3E** · Use — guided
1 There used to be a bakery in the basement. *(only* used to *— a state)*  
2 Every Saturday he would bake bread. *(or* used to bake*)*  
3 I am used to the night shift now.  
4 I got used to it.  
5 The hospital used to have its own garden. *(only* used to *— a state)*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 used to be · 2 would · 3 used to · 4 get used to · 5 Did … use · 6 use · 7 working · 8 used to have

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Eleven times a month in 1990; three now.  
2 Households buying tinned fish at least weekly rose from eighteen per cent to sixty-one.  
3 Because fresh fish has risen about forty per cent in real terms and tinned thirty-four — a gap of six points, which cannot account for a fall of eight purchases a month.  
4 In 1990, forty-one per cent of households had somebody at home between two and four in the afternoon. That figure is now eleven per cent.  
5 That the cost of fresh fish is not money but timing: it has to be bought in the morning, cooked the same day, and eaten by people who are in the house.  
6 Over sixty: young people cannot cook. Under thirty: fish is expensive.  
7 A schedule — both are a way of describing a change in the shape of the day without mentioning it.  
8 Because the canteen buys for four hundred people whose shifts are the reason the region's afternoons are empty: whatever it serves, it is serving the thing that caused the change.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**in real terms** — adjusted for inflation, so the comparison is honest · **account for** — explain the whole of · **the shape of the day** — when people are at home, awake and free · **a way of describing** — a description that does not name what it describes · **whatever it serves** — the menu is beside the point; the institution is the cause

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Six in 1994; four now.  
2 In money it has gone from half price to a subsidy of 55 per cent of a much larger number. In real terms the subsidy is worth less: the catering budget funding it has fallen 19 per cent in real terms since 2019, and the subsidy is reviewed each March.  
3 Fish of the day, cazuela, and beans and rice. *(Tinned fruit also appears on both.)*  
4 The salad bar, priced per 100 g — a category that did not exist in 1994.  
5 "A subsidised staff meal costs the hospital 2,180. The subsidy is funded from the catering budget, which has fallen 19 % in real terms since 2019. The subsidy is reviewed each March."

**6A** · Sort — a habit, a change, or a speed?
> **a speed** — overnight · gradually · steadily · constantly
> **a habit** — routine · practice · typically · tradition · custom
> **a change** — increasingly
*Increasingly* is the only one that names a direction without naming a rate, which is why it survives in writing where *gradually* would have to be defended. *Constantly* is a speed in form and a habit in use — accept it in either column with a reason.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 time · 2 rule · 3 more · 4 up · 5 into · 6 days

**6C** · Three adverbs that are not interchangeable
1 **gradually** (or *steadily*) — twenty years with stops and starts. *Overnight* would be false. 2 **overnight** — in March, with no notice; and here it is literal rather than an exaggeration. *Gradually* would contradict "with no notice". 3 **steadily** — the afternoon figure fell from 41 to 11 per cent without reversing. *Overnight* would be absurd; *gradually* would allow for stops the data does not show.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** used to *(a past state or repeated job — and she does not now)*
> **2** used to *(a state; *would* is impossible with* be*)*
> **3** used to *(present familiarity, + -ing)*
> **4** would *(or* used to *— a repeated action)*
> **5** used to *(a state; not* would*)*
> **6** 'm used to *(present familiarity)*
> **7** Did … use *(question form — no* d *on* use*)*
> **8** get used to *(the process of becoming familiar)*

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 used to be · 2 would · 3 used to · 4 get used to · 5 down · 6 staple · 7 up · 8 time · 9 have been waiting · 10 which · 11 used · 12 to replace


---

## B1.2 · Unit 2 · The Form Nobody Signs

**0B** · Guess it
The barcode scanner, which is mounted on the wall as a doorstop because it has been broken since March, and the drug cupboard, whose shelf labels have been written over by hand. Somebody has worked around a scanner that does not scan and a label that does not match the formulary — and both work-arounds held for eleven weeks.

**1B** · Sort — the event, the response, or the system?
> **the event** — incident · harm · error
> **the response** — report · disclosure · anonymous
> **the system** — blame · culture · prevent
**The one argued about in the wrong column:** *blame*. It is a property of the system — what the system does with a report — and it is always discussed as though it were a response one person chooses. That is why the signature field changed the filing rate and no policy did.

**2A** · Two milligrams 🔊 Track 2.1
**1** c) at the bedside · **2** a) porter · **3** b) not at all · **4** b) filed
**Table** — Carla: prepared the dose and did not catch it; would have checked the formulary against the label rather than the shelf; is afraid of being the person who gets named. Dr Benítez: signed the prescription; would have written the dose in words as well as figures; is afraid the investigation stops at Carla. Ignacio: read the wristband and stopped it; would have done nothing differently; is afraid of being asked why a porter was reading a wristband.

**Noticing** — Least to most formal: **2** (*If only I'd said something*) · **1** (*If I hadn't looked…*) · **3** (*But for the scanner being broken…*) · **4** (*Had it not been for Ignacio…*).

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 had not checked · 2 would have received · 3 had been · 4 been working · 5 would not have reached · 6 had acted · 7 would have been · 8 would not be

**3E** · Use — guided
1 If the scanner had not been broken, the error would not have reached the bedside.  
2 Had Ignacio not been passing, he would not have noticed. *(inverted)*  
3 If anybody had acted on the March report, the cupboard would have been relabelled.  
4 Had she not reported it, the cupboard would not have been changed. *(inverted)*  
5 If he had signed the form, it would have been investigated.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 had not looked / would have had · 2 Had … been · 3 for · 4 had said · 5 had acted / would have been · 6 Had · would not have prepared · 7 had not signed / would have happened · 8 had studied / would be *(mixed — a past condition with a present result)*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 In March, and she signed it because that is what you are told to do and because she had been in the job eight weeks and had not yet learned which instructions are real.  
2 It was relabelled in April, then relabelled back in May by somebody in pharmacy who had not been told why it had been changed and who correctly observed that the new labels did not match the formulary.  
3 She has the automatic reply: two lines long, timed 04.11.  
4 She was forty minutes past the end of a shift and thought *if I write this one, I will be the person who writes these*.  
5 That no policy needs to contain a sentence about being the person who writes these for the fear to work. The deterrent is social, and it is stronger for not being written down.  
6 A bag went up at ten times the dose and was caught at the bedside by a porter.  
7 Because thanking him would mean writing down what he was doing — reading a wristband, which is not his job.  
8 That the third form would have gone to the same inbox, arrived at four in the morning, and changed nothing. The reason to file it was never that it would have worked.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**which instructions are real** — which written rules are actually enforced · **the formulary** — the official list of drugs and doses · **the same inbox** — an address nobody is rostered to read · **past the end of a shift** — unpaid, and the point at which reporting costs something · **it would have changed nothing** — the honest admission that makes the argument stronger, not weaker

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Eight fields on the form (eleven lines in all); four are compulsory — date and time, location, what happened, what stopped it.  
2 An anonymous report is accepted and logged but cannot be followed up, because there is nobody to ask. A named report may be followed up, and the reporter is protected under the disclosure policy — in force since 2019 and never tested.  
3 55 filed; 17 anonymous.  
4 Signed: 23 of 38, 61 per cent. Anonymous: 2 of 17, 9 per cent. Overall 25 of 55, 45 per cent.  
5 Two were added this year: *Contributing factors* and *Has this happened before?* — both system questions, and both optional.

**6A** · Sort — how close, and how lucky?
> **how lucky** — fortunately · luckily · thankfully
> **how close** — nearly · almost · barely · narrowly
> **how it looks afterwards** — as it turned out · hindsight
*Barely* and *narrowly* both mean the margin was small; *barely* adds that it was nearly not enough. The second column is the one to watch in a report: all three of those words describe the writer's relief rather than the event.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 whisker · 2 minute · 3 out · 4 worse · 5 out · 6 out

**6C** · Three that look identical
1 **Luckily** — his being there was chance. *Fortunately* would claim it was a good outcome rather than an accident; *thankfully* would make it about the speaker.  
2 **Fortunately** — this is about the outcome. *Luckily* would imply the catch was chance, which insults the porter; *thankfully* is again about feelings.  
3 **Thankfully** — this sentence is openly about relief, which is the one place the word belongs. The other two would understate a family not being told.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** If anybody had acted in March, the cupboard would be right now. *(mixed — past condition, present result)*
> **2** If Ignacio had not been passing, he would not have caught it.
> **3** Had Ignacio not been there, nothing would have been caught. *(inverted)*
> **4** But for his reading the wristband, she would not be alive. *(or: Had he not read the wristband, she would not be alive.)*
> **5** If only she had filed the third form. *(regret only — no result clause)*
> **6** Had I known, I would not have prepared it. *(inverted)*
> **7** If she had not signed the first two, they would not have been investigated.
> **8** But for the broken scanner, the error would not have reached the bedside.

**7C** · Make it mean something
The first — a contrastive stress on *would* — belongs in speech, and only when somebody has just denied it; written down it reads as an argument with an absent person. The second is the neutral form and the one for a report: it states the consequence without implying anybody disputes it.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 had not looked / would have had · 2 Had … been · 3 for · 4 had said · 5 cause · 6 faith · 7 out · 8 worse · 9 used to be · 10 used to · 11 had already started · 12 must have broken


---

## B1.2 · Unit 3 · The Post That Ends on Friday

**0B** · Guess it
The locum, who is covering a consultant post he was not appointed to and is carrying an agency lanyard rather than a hospital one; and the administrator running the recruitment round, who has a finance badge and is holding the vacancy notice — the department has no HR post, so the person who costs the budget is also the person writing the advertisement.

**1B** · Sort — a thing you apply for, a thing you are given, or a thing you become?
> **a thing you apply for** — shortlist · post · vacancy
> **a thing you are given** — appointment · reference
> **a thing you become** — supervisor · trajectory · speciality · mentor
**The one that switches:** *vacancy*. For the person it is a thing to apply for; for the institution it is a thing it is given — an unfilled post arrives, it is not sought — and the whole cost argument in this unit turns on which way round the hospital is reading the word.

**2A** · Three weeks and a Friday 🔊 Track 3.1
**1** b) eleven months · **2** b) 2.0 · **3** a) three months · **4** b) the rota changed
**Table** — Dr Herrera wants Dr Benítez renewed; it costs twice the permanent salary; what she is not saying is that four rounds have produced four applicants and nobody. The finance office wants the locum stopped; it costs three months short-staffed; what it is not saying is that the surcharge on every booking is avoidable. Dr Benítez wants a permanent post with a different rota; it costs him the agency rate; what he is not saying is that nobody has asked him.

**Noticing** — Sentences 1 and 3. Each has a past condition (*had advertised*, *hadn't come*) with a present result (*would have somebody by now*, *would be three doctors short tonight*). The tell is the time adverbial in the result clause: *by now*, *tonight*.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 had been advertised · 2 would have · 3 had not come · 4 would be · 5 were · 6 would have said · 7 advertise · 8 will be

**3E** · Use — guided
1 If we had advertised in March, we would have somebody now. *(mixed — past condition, present result)*  
2 If he were permanent, he would have said something in March. *(mixed — present condition, past result)*  
3 If she had not trained in paediatrics, she would not be where she is now. *(mixed — past condition, present result)*  
4 If I were a confident speaker, I would have applied in 2019. *(mixed — present condition, past result)*  
5 If they had not cut the training budget in 2021, there would be five supervisors now. *(mixed — past condition, present result)*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 had advertised / would have · 2 were / would have said · 3 had not come / would be · 4 had not taken / would be working · 5 were / would have applied · 6 had not cut / would be · 7 advertise / will be · 8 had spoken / would have got

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Paediatrics, since she was nineteen — she had arranged her entire fifth year around it, including an elective she paid for herself.  
2 The one train to the Temuco interviews was cancelled. Standing on the platform at six in the morning she decided that arriving forty minutes late was worse than not arriving, and did not take the replacement bus.  
3 Because she thinks they would have seen her. She was twenty-four, had never been late to anything, and did not know that lateness is survivable.  
4 She took the emergency rotation that was still open, because it was the only one still open.  
5 That emergency medicine changes what a person is like — she has watched it happen to eleven others — so the person she now is was produced by the cancellation, not chosen.  
6 A candidate arrived thirty-five minutes late, apologised once, and was excellent; two of the three panellists wanted to mark her down for it.  
7 She told them about the train, and they appointed her. She has not told the candidate any of it.  
8 Open — but the strongest reading is that telling her would make the appointment a story about the writer rather than about the candidate, and would hand her a debt she did not incur. *(Accept: because the writer is not sure the story is a lesson rather than a regret.)*

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**an elective** — a placement a student arranges and often pays for · **the replacement bus** — the option that existed and was refused · **lateness is survivable** — being late does not end things, which a twenty-four-year-old did not know · **still open** — not chosen, merely left · **mark her down** — deduct for the lateness rather than judge the interview

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Consultant, emergency medicine; 4,160,000 a month.  
2 One month of locum cover is 8,341,600 — a ratio of 2.0 to the permanent post. Eleven months at that rate is 91.7 million.  
3 Eleven months; advertised four times, four applicants in all, nobody appointed.  
4 It says they are looking for "the right fit for a busy and supportive department". It does not state the rota, the number of unfilled posts, or that the department has been running short since January.  
5 The out-of-hours surcharge, 1,120,000 — applied where a booking is made with less than 72 hours' notice. All bookings in the period were.

**6A** · Sort — how formal, and how certain?
> **written** — consequently · therefore · as a result
> **very formal** — hence · accordingly · thereby
> **spoken** — so · which meant that
**The one usually got wrong:** *thereby*. It takes an *-ing* clause and attaches to the same subject — *The post was left unadvertised, thereby costing 91 million* — and learners use it as a sentence adverb meaning *therefore*, where it is simply wrong.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 result · 2 meant · 3 led · 4 off · 5 about · 6 on

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
1 **consequence** — it follows directly from the vacancy. 2 **knock-on** — nobody was looking at supervision when the budget was cut. 3 **outcome** — where the whole process ended, after four rounds.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** If they had not cut the budget, there would be five supervisors now. **Mixed A** — past condition, present result.
> **2** If he spoke Spanish, he would have got the job. **Mixed B** — present condition, past result.
> **3** If we had advertised, we would have somebody now. **Mixed A.**
> **4** If the train had run, her life would be different now. **Mixed A.**
> **5** If she had taken the bus, she would be a paediatrician now. **Mixed A.**
> **6** If I were permanent, I would have said something in March. **Mixed B.**
> **7** If I were permanent, I would say something. **Second conditional** — both halves present.
> **8** If we had advertised, we would have had somebody in June. **Third conditional** — both halves past.

**7C** · Make it mean something
What follows. *We'd have* is followed by a bare infinitive (*have somebody*), so the next word is an infinitive; *we'd advertised* is followed by a past participle. The contraction is identical and the verb after it carries the whole tense.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 had advertised / would have · 2 were / would have said · 3 for · 4 up · 5 paper · 6 result · 7 led · 8 off · 9 had not looked / would have had · 10 used to be · 11 will be living · 12 who / that


---

## B1.2 · Unit 4 · One Bed, Two Scores

**0B** · Guess it
The two sets of used gloves, the third pair still sealed, and the unused dressing pack. Two sets of gloves say two people were working; the sealed third pair says there was no third; the opened-but-unused pack says the work stopped before it began. Together they rule out a resuscitation — three or more people and an opened crash trolley — and they rule out a completed procedure.

**1B** · Sort — a thing, a relation, or a conclusion?
> **a relation** — sequence · inconsistent · consistent · angle
> **a thing** — residue · evidence · trace · timeline
> **a conclusion** — reconstruct
**The one that needs two of something else:** *inconsistent*. Nothing is inconsistent on its own — it takes two observations to make the relation, which is why a single trace is never evidence against anything. *(Accept* consistent *for the same reason.)*

**2A** · Reading the room 🔊 Track 4.1
**1** c) at least two · **2** b) started and stopped · **3** b) forty minutes · **4** a) door wedge
**Table** — Two sets of gloves: at least two people were working; rules out one person alone. The unused dressing pack: somebody started something and stopped; rules out a completed procedure, and rules out nothing having been begun. The trolley angle: it was moved from the wall and not put back; rules out an unhurried departure.

**Noticing** — Most to least certain: **2** *can't have been* (excludes absolutely) · **1** *must have been* (near certain) · **4** *unlikely to have been* (improbable, not impossible) · **3** *might well have been* (possible, strengthened).

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 must have been · 2 can't have been · 3 must mean · 4 might well have been · 5 is unlikely · 6 was

**3E** · Use — guided
1 There must have been two people.  
2 He might well have been called away.  
3 It is unlikely to have been an arrest.  
4 The coffee can't have been made after the monitor was switched on.  
5 Somebody might have come back for the phone.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 must have been · 2 can't have been · 3 might well have been · 4 is / have · 5 must have been · 6 can't have been · 7 must be *(present — her coat is here now)* · 8 probability

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Eleven variables; a number between 0 and 40.  
2 Above 28, intensive care; below 12, ward. Between the two, clinical judgement.  
3 That by handing the middle band back to a person, the system is conceding it sorts patients rather than deciding about them — it is a tool, not an oracle.  
4 About four points in either direction. So two patients both scoring 31 are indistinguishable to the instrument.  
5 Because 29 and 33 differ by four, which is inside the interval — so the instrument cannot separate them either.  
6 That the decision be made on clinical grounds and documented.  
7 That in practice the tie is broken by who arrived first, and that nobody writes that down.  
8 Not from the instrument but from the page of the manual that should exist and does not — the page saying what to do when the instrument has told you nothing.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**well validated** — tested against outcomes and shown to work · **a higher bar than it sounds** — beating most people most of the time is genuinely hard · **an oracle** — something that answers rather than sorts · **a confidence interval** — the range within which the true value probably sits · **independently** — without having discussed it with each other, which is what makes three of four significant

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Both 31. They differ on four of the eleven variables.  
2 Respiratory rate (B worse, 5 against 3); oxygen saturation (B worse, 5 against 4); systolic BP (A worse, 5 against 3); conscious level (A worse, 3 against 2). Two in each direction.  
3 "The score carries a confidence interval of +/- 4 points. Two patients whose scores differ by less than this should be regarded as indistinguishable by this instrument, and the decision made on clinical grounds and documented."  
4 Patient B: "Resp rate climbing. Score does not reflect this." Patient A: nothing.  
5 Patient B's sheet carries a time of assessment; Patient A's box was left blank. Arrival times are 03.54 (A) and 04.05 (B) — eleven minutes apart, which is the unwritten tiebreak.

**6A** · Sort — raising, lowering, or reporting?
> **reporting** — apparently · presumably · seemingly
> **lowering** — plausibly · implausibly · conceivably · arguably
> **raising** — doubtless · certainly · undoubtedly · definitely
**The two misused:** *apparently* and *seemingly*. Both report — one reports what somebody else said, the other reports an appearance — and both are routinely used as if they raised certainty. *Doubtless* is the third trap: it raises in form and weakens in use.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 suggests · 2 evidence · 3 probability · 4 well · 5 stand · 6 add

**6C** · Three that weaken what they attach to
1 *The patient was stable at four.* **Gained:** a claim somebody can check. **Lost:** the fact that this is an appearance rather than a measurement.  
2 *Nobody was called.* **Gained:** directness. **Lost:** the attribution — *apparently* was reporting somebody else, and without it the writer owns the claim.  
3 *This is the most important variable.* **Gained:** a position. **Lost:** the admission that it is contestable — and *arguably* was conceding that the writer is the one arguing.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** The monitor must have been switched on before the gloves were used.
> **2** He might well have been called away.
> **3** It is unlikely to have been an arrest.
> **4** She might have made the coffee.
> **5** There can't have been anybody else here.
> **6** There must have been two people here.
> **7** The decision was arguably made on arrival order.
> **8** The door was apparently wedged by the porter.

**7C** · Make it mean something
The contrastive *MUST* belongs in the argument about the reconstruction — it answers somebody who has just said one. The weak *must've* belongs in the reconstruction itself: it states what the evidence gives you, with nobody disagreeing in the room.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 must have been · 2 can't have been · 3 might · 4 unlikely · 5 sight · 6 for · 7 stand · 8 probability · 9 had advertised / would have · 10 had not looked / would have had · 11 must have broken · 12 is grown


---

## B1.2 · Unit 5 · Four Per Cent

**0B** · Guess it
The new ward and the corridor refurbishment. The ward: the hoarding is up, the sign gives a date in the past, and the window frames are still stacked outside. The corridor: the trolleys are parked along a wall that was supposed to have been cleared, and the floor tape marking the works boundary has been walked through and never relaid.

**1B** · Sort — a number, a date, or a belief?
> **a number** — throughput · baseline · variance · demand
> **a date** — target · milestone · deadline
> **a belief** — forecast · assumption
**The belief treated as a number:** *assumption*. The four per cent sits in the model in the same typeface as the baseline, is multiplied like a measurement, and has not been reviewed since 2019. What it costs is twelve hundred and sixty-three people by year three, and no document anywhere recording the gap.

**2A** · The three-year model 🔊 Track 5.1
**1** b) 11,000 · **2** c) 16,000 · **3** c) returned for revision · **4** c) 2019
**Table** — The finance lead says the plan is affordable; assumes four per cent; if wrong, the plan is met and the service fails. Dr Herrera says the number is wrong; assumes nine; if wrong, four months are lost to a revision. Matías says both should be submitted; assumes nothing, which is the point; if he is wrong, a one-page annex is wasted.

**Noticing** — Completed: **1** (*will have passed*) and **3** (*will have doubled*). Still running: **2** (*will be running*) and **4** (*will have had*, which is a state continuing to that point — accept either placing if the learner can say why).

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 will have passed · 2 will be running · 3 will have been doing · 4 opens · 5 will have doubled · 6 will have had · 7 will still be assuming · 8 will have run out

**3E** · Use — guided
1 will be sitting — **future continuous**: at a point inside the meeting. *Will sit* would make it a decision; *will have sat* would put it after the meeting.  
2 will have finished — **future perfect**: completed before six. *Will be finishing* would put the finishing at six.  
3 will have left — **future perfect**: before the reading. *Will leave* would make it simultaneous.  
4 will have been working — **future perfect continuous**: a duration up to June. *Will have worked* loses the continuity the *for two years* needs.  
5 will know — **future simple**: a state beginning at that point. *Will have known* would imply the knowing was over.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 will have passed · 2 will be running · 3 opens / will have doubled · 4 will have been working · 5 will know · 6 will have finished by · 7 will be presenting · 8 will have run out

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 That a department's three-year plan contains a demand assumption chosen not because anybody believes it but because it produces a plan that can be approved — and that nobody writes this down, because writing it down would end it.  
2 Because the real number produces a plan asking for money that does not exist.  
3 It is returned for revision, which costs four months.  
4 They notice that the department has been running on an approved plan for two years with no document saying what it actually needs. They do **not** notice that the assumption was wrong — everybody already knew that.  
5 Not financial: that at the end of three years there will be no record of the gap.  
6 A plan that was met, a service that is failing, and no piece of paper connecting the two.  
7 Submit the four per cent plan **and** a one-page annex describing the nine per cent world, every year.  
8 Because in four years somebody will ask when this started, and somebody ought to be able to answer.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**a quiet arrangement** — an understanding everybody acts on and nobody records · **returned for revision** — sent back, at a cost of four months · **running on an approved plan** — operating under a document that no longer describes the work · **an annex** — a short attachment that does not require the main plan to change · **connecting the two** — the missing evidence that the failure and the plan are the same story

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Baseline 7,420 at 31 March; assumed annual growth 4.0 per cent.  
2 9.1, 8.8 and 8.4 per cent — variances of +5.1, +4.8 and +4.4. Cumulative variance +14.3 points.  
3 8,347 at four per cent; 9,610 at nine — a difference of 1,263.  
4 2019. The sheet does not say by whom, which is part of the point.  
5 The growth cell: "4.0 % is the planning assumption agreed in 2019. Observed growth over the last three years has averaged 8.8 %." Twenty-six words, in six point, never quoted.

**6A** · Sort — a point, a period, or a direction?
> **a period** — meanwhile · thereafter · annually
> **a direction** — progressively · ultimately · cumulatively
> **a point** — imminently · eventually · henceforth · by · shortly
*Eventually* names a point and refuses to say which, which is why it belongs in the first column and should not survive a board paper. *Henceforth* names the point a rule starts. *Annually* is a period in form and a frequency in use — accept either.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 March · 2 year · 3 period · 4 on · 5 rate · 6 meet

**6C** · Three that look like timing and are not
1 *The ward will open in Q2 of year two.* **Gained:** a date somebody can be held to. *Ultimately* was describing an end state and promising nothing.  
2 *The list will come down by March 2029 on current trends.* **Gained:** a testable claim. *Eventually* named a time and refused to say which.  
3 *The assumption will be reviewed every March from 2026.* **Gained:** a first date and a frequency. *Henceforth* said a rule had started and left the start unnamed.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** will have doubled — **future perfect**, completed before the opening.
> **2** will have reached — **future perfect**, an event completed by a deadline.
> **3** will have presented — **future perfect**, finished before four.
> **4** will be presenting — **future continuous**, in progress at four.
> **5** will have been working — **future perfect continuous**, a duration up to June.
> **6** will be running — **future continuous**, a state of affairs rather than an event.
> **7** will know — **future simple**; a state does not take the perfect here.
> **8** will have run out — **future perfect**, completed by a date.

**7C** · Make it mean something
The weak *we'll've passed it* belongs in a board paper: it states the projection with nobody disputing it. The contrastive *WILL* is an argument with a person, and in a written projection it reads as defensiveness about a number that should be standing on its own.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 will have passed · 2 will be running · 3 opens / will have doubled · 4 will have been working · 5 trends · 6 assumption · 7 back · 8 rate · 9 must have been · 10 had advertised / would have · 11 None · 12 arrives


---

## B1.2 · Unit 6 · Four Hours on the Phone

**0B** · Guess it
**Deciding:** the clinician, who is holding the consent form and a pen, and the clinic manager, who is standing at the board with the list and the four-hour queue on it. **Being decided about:** the patient, whose chair faces the desk rather than the room; the daughter in the corridor, who is outside the door with a bag on her knees; and the interpreter, who is a telephone on a trolley and has no chair at all.

**1B** · Sort — a place, an event, or a document?
> **a place** — theatre · ward
> **an event** — procedure · admission · consultation · follow-up
> **a document** — consent · discharge · referral
**The two that are both:** *consent* and *discharge*. Each is an event (consent is given; a patient is discharged) and a document (a consent form; a discharge letter). The article usually tells you: *the consent* is the form, *consent* is the act — and *a discharge* is the event while *the discharge* is nearly always the letter. *(referral* behaves the same way and is worth accepting.)*

**2A** · Her daughter is in the corridor 🔊 Track 6.1
**1** c) four hours · **2** a) sixteen · **3** c) the patient · **4** a) today, not urgent
**Table** — The patient wants her daughter to interpret; the policy forbids family members; she is worried about waiting again and losing the slot. Dr Benítez wants to take consent today; the policy requires a registered interpreter; he is worried about taking consent that is not really informed. Rosa wants the telephone service used; the policy is on her side; she is worried that the clinic will overrun and that six other patients will be sent home.

**Noticing** — Passives: **1**, **2**, **3**. Causative: **4** (*She's having it done*). The passive says the subject received the action without saying who did it; the causative says the subject arranged for somebody else to do it — the responsibility stays with her.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 was referred · 2 should have been seen · 3 can be done · 4 is having · 5 is not allowed · 6 requires · 7 has been made · 8 has been explained

**3E** · Use — guided
1 She will be seen by a consultant.  
2 She should have been told in March.  
3 She is having her transport arranged by the hospital. *(or: She is having her transport arranged.)*  
4 She is not permitted to have her daughter interpret.  
5 She was made to wait four hours.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 was referred · 2 should have been seen · 3 can be done · 4 is having · 5 is not allowed · 6 was made · 7 requires · 8 got … sent

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 That the policy is correct — because the piece that writes itself is the one about heartless bureaucracy, and that piece would be wrong.  
2 A daughter will soften a prognosis; a son will omit a question about alcohol; a sixteen-year-old will be asked to say *terminal* to her own mother and will be in that room for the rest of her life.  
3 Four hours and eleven minutes.  
4 For anything serious they wait. It costs four real hours, a clinic that runs over, and other patients seen late.  
5 For routine consultations they use the family member — and they do not record it, because recording it is a breach.  
6 It protects patients in serious conversations, and it produces a permanent, undocumented, unmeasurable practice in routine ones.  
7 That the queue is not a failure attached to a good policy: a rule that cannot be followed is in practice a rule about what gets written down, so the four hours are the policy's real content.  
8 Because the obvious article blames the rule, and the rule is right. The problem is the four hours, which no article about bureaucracy would mention.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**the piece that writes itself** — the easy, familiar article nobody has to think to write · **soften a prognosis** — make bad news sound better than it is · **a median wait** — the middle wait, so half of all waits are longer · **the clinic runs over** — appointments slip and later patients are seen late · **a breach** — a recorded failure to follow policy, with consequences for the person who records it

**5E** · The counter-text
1 It forbids any family member, including adults, from interpreting in clinical consultations. Two exceptions: an emergency ("risk to life"), and a patient refusing an interpreter. Both must be recorded.  
2 Median 4 h 11 min; ninetieth percentile 7 h 40 min.  
3 3,140 requests; 1,870 abandoned before connection — an abandonment rate of 60 per cent.  
4 Any use of a family member outside the two exceptions is a breach and must be recorded on the clinical record. *(Four breaches were recorded last year, against 1,870 abandoned calls.)*  
5 "An emergency is a situation in which delay would present a risk to life. Clinic overrun, patient distress and staff availability do not constitute an emergency." It does not cover the routine consultation, which is where the practice actually is.

**6A** · Sort — bare infinitive, *to*-infinitive, or past participle?
> **to-infinitive** — require · allow · permit · entitle · cause · oblige
> **past participle** — have · get
> **bare infinitive** — make · let
**The two that move:** *make* takes a bare infinitive in the active (*made her wait*) and a *to*-infinitive in the passive (*was made to wait*). *have* and *get* take a past participle in the causative and a *to*-infinitive in other senses (*got her to sign*) — so *get* is arguable in two columns.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 done · 2 sent · 3 to · 4 entitled / required · 5 behalf · 6 to

**6C** · Three that look like permission and are not
1 **entitled** — a right. *Obliged* or *required* would turn her right into her duty, which is the opposite.  
2 **required** (or *obliged*) — a duty imposed by the policy. *Entitled* would make the record optional.  
3 **obliged** — the moral flavour is the point: there is no duty, and no rule, and that is the finding. *Required* would be factually the same and would lose the reproach.

**6D** · The fixed expression
The words do not change; the stress and the context do. Spoken as a decision, the stress falls on *out* and the sentence is about an arrangement the speaker made. Spoken as a misfortune, the stress falls on *had* and the sentence is about something done to them. English leaves the two identical on the page, which is why a clinical record has to say who decided.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** It can be done under local. **Modal passive.**
> **2** She was made to wait. **Passive** — and *make* takes *to* in the passive.
> **3** I got my notes sent to the clinic. **Causative with *get*** — informal.
> **4** She should have been seen in March. **Modal perfect passive.**
> **5** She is having it done on Thursday. **Causative with *have*.**
> **6** Nothing was explained to her. **Passive.**
> **7** She was referred by her own doctor. **Passive**, agent kept.
> **8** Her daughter is not permitted to interpret. **Passive.**

**7C** · Make it mean something
Neutral stress — *She had her hair **cut*** — is the arrangement: she went to a hairdresser. Stress on *HAD* is the misfortune: somebody made her, and the stress is the only thing marking it. The ambiguity is real in English and is why the causative needs an agent in writing whenever the difference matters.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 was referred · 2 should have been seen · 3 can be done · 4 is having · 5 was made · 6 consent · 7 forward · 8 to · 9 will have passed · 10 must have been · 11 is grown · 12 told


---

## B1.2 · Unit 7 · Eleven Nights

**0B** · Guess it
The coat on the back of the same chair and the mug on the window ledge. The coat has not moved through eleven shift changes — the hook beside it is empty every night — and the mug has a ring of eleven tide marks dried inside it. Both say that one person has been coming back to the same square metre without going home properly.

**1B** · Sort — about the body, the mind, or the situation?
> **the body** — dependence · relapse · craving · abstinence
> **the mind** — distraction · willpower · attention
> **the situation** — trigger · substitute
**The one misfiled:** *willpower*. It is described as a quantity in the mind and is almost entirely situational — the same person has plenty of it on night two and none on night nine, and nothing inside them has changed except how many hours they have been awake.

**2A** · Eleven nights 🔊 Track 7.1
**1** c) twelve · **2** a) money · **3** a) both · **4** b) vague
**Table** — Carla says she is fine and chose it; she is actually worried that she cannot remember the second check. Dr Herrera says the rota permits it; she is actually worried that permitting is being read as approving. The rota policy says twelve with a recommended seven; what it is worried about is being blamed, which is why approval may be given retrospectively.

**Noticing** — **1** *stopped to think* = paused in order to think. **2** *stopped thinking* = the thinking ended. **3** *remember checking* = a memory of a past act. **4** *remembered to check* = did not forget a duty.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 checking · 2 checking · 3 to do · 4 thinking · 5 stopping to notice · 6 drinking · 7 staying · 8 changing · 9 to raise

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *She stopped smoking* (the smoking ended) / *She stopped to smoke* (she paused in order to).  
2 *I remember locking the door* (a memory) / *I remembered to lock the door* (I did not forget the duty).  
3 *Try asking somebody else* (an experiment — it might work) / *Try to ask somebody else* (make the effort — it may be difficult).  
4 *He went on explaining* (continued the same explanation) / *He went on to explain* (moved to a new point).  
5 *I regret telling him* (I did it and wish I had not) / *I regret to tell you* (a formal preface to bad news I am about to give).

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 thinking · 2 to think · 3 checking · 4 to check · 5 changing · 6 talking · 7 to say · 8 changing *(or* to be changed*)*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 It was four; somebody was off; she picked up two more because the night rate is a third more and she has a daughter at university in Santiago.  
2 Around night six she stopped thinking about it. The distinction is between stopping caring and stopping thinking — it went from a thing she was doing to the shape of the week.  
3 Somebody asked if she was all right and she said she was fine. She remembers saying it and remembers meaning it.  
4 At two she remembers checking a drug chart — a memory of an act. After that she does not remember checking it again, believes she did, and cannot say that she did.  
5 Because the absence of harm is what prevents the system from looking: if nothing happened there is no form, no review and no change.  
6 A form, a review, and the rota would have been looked at.  
7 You are not going to notice night seven; nobody notices night seven; get somebody else to count for you.  
8 Not your hands and not your judgement — your ability to be the person who is counting.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**picked up two** — took two extra shifts that were not hers · **the shape of the week** — the default pattern, no longer a decision · **a defence** — something that would excuse her, which she says this is not · **looked at** — formally reviewed · **the person who is counting** — the one keeping track, which is the capacity that fails first

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Twelve. Seven is the recommended maximum, and the two numbers sit one line apart.  
2 Voluntary additional nights above seven require the approval of the department lead. Approval may be given retrospectively, and nothing requires the lead to be told in advance.  
3 Nights 1–4 and 12 were on the printed rota; 5–6 and 7–9 were approved retrospectively; 10–11 have no approval recorded at all. Only four of the eleven were ever on a printed rota.  
4 A minimum rest of 48 hours after a run. Between nights she averaged 9 h 20 min, including the journey.  
5 Beside night 8, in biro: "C says she is fine. — asked 02.40". Somebody did ask, at twenty to three, and wrote it down.

**6A** · Sort — *-ing*, *to*, or both with a change of meaning?
> **both, with a change** — go on · try · forget · stop · prefer · remember · need
> **to** — manage · decide
> **-ing** — consider · avoid · resent · enjoy
**The two with almost no change:** *prefer* (*prefer walking* / *prefer to walk*) and *need* in its ordinary sense (*need to change it* / *needs changing*, where the second is passive in meaning). Everything else in the first column changes meaning sharply.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 on · 2 off · 3 to · 4 on · 5 up · 6 off

**6C** · The three that are nearly a rule
The rule: *-ing* points at something already real, *to* at something intended. Model pairs — *I stopped cycling to work* / *I stopped to buy milk*; *I remember posting it* / *I remembered to post it*; *I will never forget meeting her* / *I forgot to meet her*. The rule holds for all three, and the usual objection — *I stopped to think, which was intended* — is actually the rule working: the thinking was intended, and the stopping was for it.

**6D** · The fixed expression
**Honest:** *The rota went past seven and I knew it on night eight. I meant to. I did not, and the reason is that saying it would have left two shifts uncovered.* **Not honest:** *Somebody should have raised the rota two years ago. I meant to. It is really a matter for the department lead.* The second sentence is the same two words doing the opposite job: it converts an intention into a defence.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** locking — a memory of having done it.
> **2** to finish — make the effort; it may be difficult.
> **3** working — he continued the same activity.
> **4** to lock — the duty was not forgotten.
> **5** smoking — the smoking ended.
> **6** moving — an experiment; it might work.
> **7** cleaning — passive in meaning (*needs to be cleaned*).
> **8** to buy — she paused in order to.

**7C** · Make it mean something
*I didn't remember to check* is a confession: it says the duty was not done. *I don't remember checking* is a gap: it says only that the memory is missing. In a note the second is the one to write, because it is the one she can be sure is true — and the process note in Part 9 keeps exactly that sentence.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 thinking · 2 to think · 3 checking · 4 to check · 5 down · 6 turkey · 7 square · 8 off · 9 is having · 10 will have passed · 11 to replace · 12 waiting


---

## B1.2 · Unit 8 · Which Patients, Exactly

**0B** · Guess it
The finance screen and the clinical coding screen. Both are showing the same Tuesday and the totals differ by about four hundred, because finance counts a day case as an admission and coding does not. Each is correct inside its own definition, and neither screen says which definition it is using.

**1B** · Sort — the thing, the rule, or the problem?
> **the thing** — category · dataset · record
> **the problem** — ambiguity · overlap · abstraction
> **the rule** — criterion · classification · boundary
**The one that exists only because of carelessness:** *ambiguity*. A criterion and a boundary drawn precisely leave none; *the patients* is ambiguous because nobody wrote down which patients, and the ambiguity is the residue of two rules that were never reconciled.

**2A** · The patients 🔊 Track 8.1
**1** b) 38 to 58 · **2** c) nowhere · **3** c) the patients · **4** c) nobody owns the report
**Table** — Finance reads *the patients* as everybody with a bed number: 1,740 people, 41 days. Clinical coding reads it as overnight admissions only: 1,340, 47 days. The ward reads it as whoever was on the board at 08.00: 1,610, 38 days. The regional office reads it as the coding population but counts from referral received: 1,340, 58 days.

**Everybody:** sentence 1 (*Patients wait too long* — zero article, generic). **A group:** sentence 2 (*The patients waited 41 days*). **A type:** sentence 3 (*A patient waits 41 days*). **One person:** sentence 4 (*The patient waited 41 days*).

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 — *(zero)* · 2 The · 3 the · 4 the · 5 a · 6 the · 7 The · 8 the

**3E** · Use — guided
1 Patients are waiting longer. *(zero article — generic)*  
2 The 1,340 patients in this report waited 41 days. *(definite, with the population named)*  
3 Every patient is entitled to an interpreter. *(or* A patient is entitled… *as a rule)*  
4 The patient is not consulted. *(definite singular — the category, formal register)*  
5 Such patients are excluded. *(pointing back to the previous sentence)*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 — *(zero)* · 2 The · 3 A · 4 The · 5 Such · 6 The *(four departments)* — accept *The four* · 7 the latter · 8 For

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Whether day cases should be counted as admissions.  
2 Finance: the tariff is paid on an admission basis, so a day case not counted is a day case not paid for. Clinical coding: the national standard defines an admission as requiring an overnight stay, and a local variation would make the hospital's returns incomparable with every other hospital's.  
3 That both positions were correct.  
4 That each department would keep its own definition for its own purposes, to be reviewed when a single reporting system arrived. Mr Vergara pointed out that this effectively meant the hospital had two admission figures.  
5 That it had always had two admission figures and the meeting had simply written that down. The force of it is that the ambiguity predates the decision — the minute records a state of affairs rather than creating one.  
6 The information manager was to prepare a note setting out both definitions for circulation to all departments.  
7 No note was circulated. The single reporting system arrived in 2019 and uses the finance definition without saying so. The coding definition remains in use for national returns. Item 7 has not been reviewed.  
8 Both. The finance definition is in the reporting system; the coding definition is in the national returns. The system does **not** say which it is using.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**on an admission basis** — paid per admission, so the count is the money · **a local variation** — a definition used here and nowhere else · **incomparable** — not able to be set beside another hospital's figures · **effectively meant** — the practical consequence, whatever the minute says · **without stating that it does so** — the system makes a choice and does not declare it, which is the whole finding

**5E** · The counter-text
1 "The patients waited an average of 41 days from referral to first appointment." It does not say which patients, how they were counted, or from which referral date.  
2 Finance 41 days (1,740 people); clinical coding 47 (1,340); the ward 38 (1,610); the regional return 58 (1,340). The range is 38 to 58 days.  
3 Nowhere on the page, and nowhere in the report. The regional difference — counting from referral received rather than referral dated, worth about eleven days — is documented in the regional guidance and nowhere here.  
4 Clinical coding's population (1,340), with the regional counting rule, giving 58 days. The headline uses finance.  
5 "Which patients? — asked 2011, 2016, 2019, and now," in the information manager's hand.

**6A** · Sort — narrowing, generalising, or pointing back?
> **generalising** — as a whole · generic
> **pointing back** — such · the latter · the former · respective
> **narrowing** — given · any · specific · certain · particular
*Any* narrows in a formula (*any given month*) and generalises in ordinary use (*any patient may ask*), so expect it in two columns. *Certain* narrows in form and withholds in fact — it is the one 6C is about.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 aggregate · 2 of · 3 of · 4 question · 5 for · 6 to

**6C** · Three that look like precision
1 **Certain** — some patients, and the sentence is not saying which, which is exactly the fault the unit is about. *Particular* would promise to name them; *given* would make no sense.  
2 **given** — specified, in a formula: for whichever month you specify. *Certain* would mean some months and not others; *particular* would mean one named month.  
3 **particular** — this one as opposed to the other three. *Certain* would be evasive; *given* would be formulaic.

**6D** · The fixed expression
**Welcome:** *The report says the patients waited 41 days. Finance has 41 and coding has 47.* — **Which patients, exactly?** — *Then we should put the population in the sentence before this goes to the board.* **Unwelcome:** *The paper is agreed and we are twenty minutes over. Any final comments?* — **Which patients, exactly?** — *Can we take that offline?*

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** A — the indefinite singular, meaning a representative type.
> **2** The — the definite singular, meaning the category; formal register.
> **3** The respective — or *The four*; each department its own.
> **4** the latter — the second of two already named.
> **5** given — specified, in a formula.
> **6** The — this group, already identified.
> **7** — *(zero article)* — generic; patients in general.
> **8** Such — of the kind just described.

**7C** · Make it mean something
*It's a definition* — one of several. In the 2011 minutes that is the true one: paragraph 7.4 agrees that both positions are correct, and 7.5 lets both stand. The 2019 reporting system behaves as though the second were true, and says nothing.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 — *(zero)* · 2 The · 3 A · 4 definition · 5 purposes · 6 under · 7 down · 8 of · 9 thinking · 10 is having · 11 — *(zero)* · 12 None


---

## B1.2 · Unit 9 · True and Misleading

**0B** · Guess it
The bar chart with a truncated axis starting at 85 per cent, and the same data as a histogram of waiting times. The truncated bar makes a two-point rise look like a doubling; the histogram shows the long tail. **The honest one is the histogram**, because it shows the four hundred people the percentage removes.

**1B** · Sort — a measure, a population, or a shape?
> **a population** — sample · denominator · cohort
> **a shape** — distribution · outlier · trend
> **a measure** — percentile · rate · median
**The measure reported without its population:** *rate*. A rate is a fraction and the denominator is half of it; published alone it can be made to say almost anything, which is why 310 returned referrals can leave the headline and nobody notices the number move.

**2A** · Ninety-four per cent 🔊 Track 9.1
**1** b) 400 · **2** a) nine months · **3** c) right · **4** a) the median
**Table** — The communications lead wants the 94 per cent alone; because it is the form the region asks for and the hospital ranks fourth of twelve; afraid of being the only hospital publishing a median and looking worse than hospitals performing worse. Dr Herrera wants the median beside it; because the three pathways are clinical, not statistical; afraid the pathway with no consultant disappears again. The information manager wants the denominator published; because 310 referrals were returned and are in no published number; afraid of being asked in March why nobody said so.

**Most to least reassuring:** **2** *the vast majority* · **1** *most of them* · **3** *roughly nine out of ten* · **4** *all but about four hundred* — which is the only one that makes the reader count the exception.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 Most · 2 vast · 3 roughly · 4 about · 5 All · 6 marginally · 7 such · 8 works

**3E** · Use — guided
1 The vast majority are seen in six weeks. **Changes:** the number disappears, and *vast* does work no figure is supporting.  
2 All but about four hundred are seen in six weeks. **Changes:** the exception becomes a countable group of people rather than a residue.  
3 A significant minority are not. **Changes:** *significant* asserts importance the sentence has not shown; it is a claim, not a measurement.  
4 Next to none of the four hundred were seen in under a year. **Changes:** the honest hedge on two out of four hundred — it concedes the two without inflating them.  
5 The figure has risen marginally. **Changes:** 2.1 points becomes the tightest honest hedge there is; anything stronger would be a claim.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 Most · 2 majority … are *(or* is*, with a singular reading)* · 3 is · 4 All · 5 Next · 6 marginally · 7 out · 8 handful / minority

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 That the figure is not wrong — they have checked it and it is right. It matters because it removes the arithmetic argument and leaves only the real one.  
2 About four hundred; their median wait is nine months.  
3 They are concentrated on three pathways, not spread across the service. One of the three is the pathway with no consultant.  
4 Not about arithmetic — about what a number is for: every publication of 94 per cent tells four hundred people that the service is meeting a target it is not meeting for them.  
5 That the figure be published with the median wait of the excluded group in the same sentence. They explicitly refuse a footnote.  
6 That 94 per cent is the form the regional office asks for and the form in which the hospital is compared with eleven others; publishing a median would make it the only one doing so and it would look worse than hospitals performing worse.  
7 That comparison requires a common form, so a hospital that publishes more looks worse. It is real because the ranking is produced from the common figure and nobody adjusts for candour.  
8 That it is easy to say in a letter, and that he will be asked about it in March.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**distributed across** — spread evenly over the whole service · **concentrated on** — bunched into a few places, which is what makes it fixable · **what a number is for** — whether it informs or reassures · **in that form** — in the shape the comparison requires · **easy to say in a letter** — the writer's own hedge on his own promise

**5E** · The counter-text
1 94.0 per cent; the denominator is 6,700 accepted referrals. 7,010 were received and 310 returned, so the returned referrals are in no published number.  
2 The whole list: median 11 days, mean 19. The 402: median 9 months, mean 11 months.  
3 Fifty-one have waited more than two years. 340 of the 402 are on three pathways, and 212 of those are on the single pathway that has had no consultant since January.  
4 The hospital is fourth of twelve on 94 per cent; the range across the twelve is 88 to 97 per cent; none of the twelve publishes a median.  
5 "Over 2 years: 51." Struck out for the board pack and reinstated by the chief executive on 14 October.

**6A** · Sort — tighter than, looser than, or bigger than?
> **bigger than** — substantially · notably · considerably · largely
> **looser than** — comparatively · broadly · approximately · roughly
> **tighter than** — marginally · slightly · somewhat
**The four misused:** *substantially*, *considerably*, *largely* and *notably*. All four are written as though they were hedges and all four make the claim larger, which is why a reader asks for a number immediately after any of them.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 region · 2 part · 3 none · 4 bulk · 5 to / take · 6 out

**6C** · Three that are not hedges
1 *Waits have increased by 2.1 points.* **Given up:** the sense of alarm — and gained a number a reader can check.  
2 *The figure is marginally better than last year.* **Given up:** the claim of a real improvement, which the two points never supported.  
3 *340 of the 402 are on three pathways.* **Given up:** nothing at all; *largely* was standing in for a figure that is more persuasive than the adverb.

**6D** · The fixed expression
A listener cannot tell: the phrase is identical whether you have counted or not. What makes it honest in the second case is adding the basis — *about a third, give or take; I have not counted, that is from the shape of the list* — which costs one clause and is the whole difference between a hedge and a guess wearing one.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** marginally — the tightest honest hedge available.
> **2** the bulk of the delay sits on three pathways — and give the number, 340 of 402.
> **3** 6,700, give or take forty.
> **4** a significant minority — but only if the significance is then shown.
> **5** next to none — the honest hedge on a number that is not zero.
> **6** all but about four hundred — counting the exception.
> **7** roughly eleven per cent below — or *in the region of*.
> **8** all but about four hundred — counting the exception.

**7C** · Make it mean something
The patients' group writes the stressed one: contrastive stress on *MOST* implies a remainder and makes the reader ask how many. The press office writes the flat one, where *most* passes as a quantity rather than a comparison. Same four words.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 Most · 2 are · 3 All · 4 Next · 5 majority · 6 doubled · 7 off · 8 to · 9 The · 10 thinking · 11 Neither · 12 so

## B1.2 · Unit 10 · Where You Are Needed

**0B** · Guess it
The laminated card of extension numbers taped inside a cupboard door, and the handwritten list of who actually answers them, stuck beside it. Both were made by junior staff: the official directory is on the intranet and is two reorganisations out of date, so somebody printed it, and somebody else corrected it in pen.

**1B** · Sort — what you are taught, what you absorb, or what you are left to work out?
> **what you are left to work out** — insight · ignorance · blind spot
> **what you absorb** — mentorship · apprenticeship · confidence
> **what you are taught** — curriculum · competence · feedback
**The one that depends on your supervisor:** *mentorship*. With one supervisor it is something you absorb without noticing; with another it is the thing you are left to work out, because nobody models it — which is exactly the finding in paragraph 4.4 about saying *I don't know*.

**2A** · Needed, or good 🔊 Track 10.1
**1** c) twenty-two · **2** c) a lot · **3** a) deliberately not clear · **4** a) what nobody told her
**Table** — Matías says he will decide by March; wishes somebody would simply tell him; will not say that he already knows which one he wants. Dr Herrera says both are defensible; wishes she had been told something at his age; will not say *take the shortage post*, because she thinks the advice would be heard as an instruction. Carla says nobody drew her a map in her first year; wishes somebody had; will not say that she stopped reporting things in her second year.

**Now:** 1 (*I wish I knew*) and 4 (*I'd rather somebody just said it*) — a past-form verb with present reference. **The past:** 2 (*I wish I'd known*) and 3 (*If only somebody had told me*) — the past perfect, one step further back.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 knew · 2 had told · 3 did not change · 4 would stop · 5 said · 6 asked · 7 could · 8 had thought

**3E** · Use — guided
1 I wish I knew what I wanted.  
2 I wish somebody had told me. *(or* If only somebody had told me.*)*  
3 I wish he would stop saying that.  
4 I wish I could tell you.  
5 If only I had asked her.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 knew · 2 had told · 3 would stop · 4 could · 5 had asked · 6 did not say · 7 asked · 8 had not been

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 211 doctors; eleven gave clinical answers.  
2 **Who to ask** — about half of all answers. Several described spending twenty to fifty minutes locating a person who could answer a question that took ninety seconds to answer. One wrote: *I wish somebody had drawn me a map of who knows what.*  
3 Either a senior colleague says *I don't know* in front of them in the first fortnight, or nobody does.  
4 That it is almost entirely a matter of modelling, and the modelling either happens in the first two weeks or does not happen at all.  
5 **What is normal** — being unable to tell, for about a year, whether a thing that felt wrong was wrong or was simply how hospitals are. Several said they had stopped reporting things in their second year, not because they had stopped noticing but because they had stopped being able to tell.  
6 The first — *who to ask*. It costs almost nothing and is the one nobody does.  
7 190 of 211 responses described something the institution could have provided and did not, while the institution has asked three times whether the clinical content is adequate. The force is that the question being asked is the one the survey has already answered — five per cent of responses were about clinical content.  
8 Because the juxtaposition is the comment. Saying anything further would weaken it, and the phrase marks that the authors know exactly what they have just done.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**locating a person** — finding, with difficulty, somebody who knows · **a matter of modelling** — learned by seeing it done, not by instruction · **what is normal** — the baseline against which anything can be judged wrong · **stopped being able to tell** — lost the capacity to judge, which is worse than losing the will · **without comment** — a formal silence that is itself the loudest sentence in the extract

**5E** · The counter-text
1 820 hours across fourteen domains.  
2 *Who to ask* (49 per cent), *how to say I do not know* (27 per cent) and *what is normal* (19 per cent) — 95 per cent of the responses, and none of them is in the curriculum.  
3 "Working in a team" — two hours, in week nine.  
4 Added: prescribing safety, +40 hours. Removed: induction week, −16 hours.  
5 Beside "Working in a team": "2 hours. In week nine. By which time you have either worked it out or stopped asking."

**6A** · Sort — a wish, a preference, or a hypothesis?
> **a hypothesis** — supposedly · suppose · hypothetically · admittedly
> **a preference** — frankly · ideally · rather · preferably
> **a wish** — regrettably · wish · if only
**The two that are not about the future:** *supposedly* and *admittedly*. Both look like hypothesis markers and both point backwards — one at somebody else's claim, the other at a concession the speaker has already made. *frankly* is the third trap: it announces a manner, not a preference.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 only · 2 rather · 3 we · 4 if · 5 world · 6 back

**6C** · Three that are not hedges
1 **Supposedly** — the sentence reports the institution's claim and doubts it. *Admittedly* would concede it, which is the opposite; *frankly* would be rude about something nobody disputes.  
2 **Admittedly** — a concession against the speaker. *Supposedly* would be absurd about one's own actions; *frankly* would make an admission sound like an attack.  
3 **Frankly** — the sentence is impolite about the review and announces it. *Supposedly* would attribute the claim elsewhere; *admittedly* would make it a concession, which it is not.

**6D** · The fixed expression
**Would have listened:** *If only somebody had told me. I spent fourteen months deciding that the thing I noticed in week three was normal.* **Would not have listened:** *If only somebody had told me. They did, in the first fortnight, and I remember thinking it was the kind of thing people say.* The second is the honest version, and it is the one almost nobody writes.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** I wish I knew.
> **2** I wish somebody had told me. *(or* If only somebody had told me.*)*
> **3** Suppose we asked them.
> **4** I wish I could help.
> **5** If only I had asked. *(the* if only *carries the force)*
> **6** I wish she would stop changing it.
> **7** I wish I had not been so sure in my first year.
> **8** I'd rather you didn't say anything.

**7C** · Make it mean something
*I **WISH** I'd known* is the defence: the stress answers an implication that you did know, and the sentence is about the speaker's state rather than the missing information. *I wish I'd **KNOWN*** puts the news on the knowing, which is the version that is actually about the gap.

**12A** · Recycle — twelve items
1 knew · 2 had told · 3 would stop · 4 had asked · 5 rather · 6 depth · 7 job · 8 hindsight · 9 are · 10 had advertised / would have · 11 had not looked / would have had · 12 were
